lvl1[1]lvl2[2]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]PEMDAS with Exponents{note:"Exponents are evaluated before multiplication and division.

Example 1:
2³ + 4
- Exponent first: 2³ = 8
- Then addition: 8 + 4 = 12

Example 2:
2 × 4²
- Exponent first: 4² = 16
- Then multiplication: 2 × 16 = 32

Example 3:
(2 × 4)²
- Parentheses first: 2 × 4 = 8
- Then exponent: 8² = 64

Example 4:
5 + 3² × 2
- Exponent: 3² = 9
- Multiplication: 9 × 2 = 18
- Addition: 5 + 18 = 23

Example 5:
4³ - 2²
- Exponent: 4³ = 64, 2² = 4
- Subtraction: 64 - 4 = 60

Example 6:
3 × 2³ + 4
- Exponent: 2³ = 8
- Multiplication: 3 × 8 = 24
- Addition: 24 + 4 = 28"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[2]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Complex PEMDAS Examples{note:"Complex expressions using all operations.

Example 1:
3 × (2 + 4)² - 6 ÷ 3
- Parentheses: 2 + 4 = 6
- Exponent: 6² = 36
- Multiplication: 3 × 36 = 108
- Division: 6 ÷ 3 = 2
- Subtraction: 108 - 2 = 106

Example 2:
(8 - 3)² × 2 + 10
- Parentheses: 8 - 3 = 5
- Exponent: 5² = 25
- Multiplication: 25 × 2 = 50
- Addition: 50 + 10 = 60

Example 3:
4 + 2 × [12 ÷ (3 + 1)]
- Inner parentheses: 3 + 1 = 4
- Division: 12 ÷ 4 = 3
- Multiplication: 2 × 3 = 6
- Addition: 4 + 6 = 10

Example 4:
3² + 4 × (5 - 2)
- Parentheses: 5 - 2 = 3
- Exponent: 3² = 9
- Multiplication: 4 × 3 = 12
- Addition: 9 + 12 = 21

Example 5:
6² ÷ (2 × 3) - 1
- Parentheses: 2 × 3 = 6
- Exponent: 6² = 36
- Division: 36 ÷ 6 = 6
- Subtraction: 6 - 1 = 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[2]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]PEMDAS with Fractions{note:"Fractions and mixed numbers follow the same order.

Example 1:
(3/4 + 1/2) × 4
- Parentheses: 3/4 + 1/2 = 3/4 + 2/4 = 5/4
- Multiplication: 5/4 × 4 = 5

Example 2:
2 + 1/2 × 4
- Multiplication: 1/2 × 4 = 2
- Addition: 2 + 2 = 4

Example 3:
(2 + 1/2) × 4
- Parentheses: 2 + 1/2 = 5/2
- Multiplication: 5/2 × 4 = 10

Example 4:
12 ÷ (3/4)
- Division: 12 × 4/3 = 16

Example 5:
1/2 + 3/4 × 2
- Multiplication: 3/4 × 2 = 6/4 = 3/2
- Addition: 1/2 + 3/2 = 4/2 = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[2]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Common PEMDAS Mistakes{note:"Avoid these common order of operations errors.

Mistake 1: Multiplication before division left to right
8 ÷ 2(2 + 2)
- Correct: 8 ÷ 2 × 4 = 4 × 4 = 16
- Wrong: 8 ÷ (2 × 4) = 8 ÷ 8 = 1

Mistake 2: Addition before multiplication
3 + 5 × 2
- Correct: 3 + 10 = 13
- Wrong: 8 × 2 = 16

Mistake 3: Exponent before negative sign
-3²
- Correct: -(3²) = -9
- Wrong: (-3)² = 9

Mistake 4: Division before exponent in certain cases
6 ÷ 2³
- Correct: 6 ÷ 8 = 0.75
- Wrong: (6÷2)³ = 3³ = 27

Mistake 5: Parentheses omitted
8 ÷ 2(2+2) vs 8 ÷ 2 × (2+2)
- 8 ÷ 2(2+2): ambiguous notation
- 8 ÷ 2 × (2+2): 16
- 8 ÷ (2(2+2)): 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]Fractions and Decimals{note:"Fractions and decimals are alternative representations of numbers that are not whole. They are essential for expressing parts of a whole, precise measurement, and proportional reasoning."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]Proper Fractions{note:"A proper fraction is a fraction where the numerator (top number) is less than the denominator (bottom number), so its value is less than 1. Examples: 1/2, 3/4, 5/8, 2/3. Proper fractions represent parts of a whole. They are the most common type of fraction used in everyday contexts like recipes, measurements, and probability."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples of proper fractions and their applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Identifying Proper Fractions{note:"Proper fractions have numerator < denominator.

Examples:
- 1/2 (half)
- 1/4 (quarter)
- 3/4 (three-quarters)
- 5/6 (five-sixths)
- 7/12 (seven-twelfths)
- 2/5 (two-fifths)
- 3/8 (three-eighths)
- 11/16 (eleven-sixteenths)

Each represents a part of a whole less than 1.

On number line:
- Proper fractions lie between 0 and 1
- 1/2 is halfway between 0 and 1
- 3/4 is three-quarters of the way from 0 to 1
- 1/8 is closer to 0 than to 1"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Visual Representations{note:"Proper fractions represent parts of a whole visually.

Pizza example:
- A pizza cut into 8 slices, you eat 3 slices = 3/8 of the pizza
- A pizza cut into 12 slices, you take 5 = 5/12
- A pizza cut into 6 slices, 2 slices eaten = 2/6 = 1/3

Cake example:
- A cake cut into 12 pieces, you take 5 = 5/12
- A cake cut into 10 pieces, you take 3 = 3/10

Other visual examples:
- A ruler: 9 inches of 16 inches = 9/16 of a foot
- A glass: 3/4 full (3 parts out of 4)
- A pie chart: a sector representing 3/8 of the whole
- A class: 17 out of 25 students present = 17/25"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Comparing Proper Fractions{note:"Proper fractions can be compared using common denominators or decimals.

Examples:
- 3/4 is larger than 1/2 (0.75 > 0.5)
- 5/8 = 0.625, 3/4 = 0.75 → 5/8 < 3/4
- 7/12 = 0.583, 2/3 = 0.667 → 7/12 < 2/3
- 3/5 = 0.6, 5/8 = 0.625 → 3/5 < 5/8
- 11/16 = 0.6875, 2/3 = 0.667 → 11/16 > 2/3

Using common denominator:
- Compare 2/3 and 3/4: common denominator 12
  2/3 = 8/12, 3/4 = 9/12 → 8/12 < 9/12 → 2/3 < 3/4
- Compare 5/8 and 7/12: common denominator 24
  5/8 = 15/24, 7/12 = 14/24 → 5/8 > 7/12

Cross multiplication:
- 3/4 vs 2/3: 3×3=9, 4×2=8 → 3/4 > 2/3
- 5/7 vs 4/5: 5×5=25, 7×4=28 → 5/7 < 4/5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Real-World Applications{note:"Proper fractions appear frequently in everyday life.

Recipes:
- 1/2 cup of sugar
- 3/4 teaspoon of salt
- 2/3 cup of flour
- 1/8 teaspoon of cinnamon
- 1/4 cup of oil

Measurements:
- 5/8 inch (slightly more than half an inch)
- 7/16 inch (slightly less than half an inch)
- 3/4 of a mile
- 1/3 of an hour = 20 minutes

Probability:
- Probability of getting heads on a coin toss: 1/2
- Probability of rolling a 6 on a die: 1/6
- Probability of drawing a heart from a deck: 13/52 = 1/4
- Probability of drawing a red card: 26/52 = 1/2

Time:
- 3/4 of an hour = 45 minutes
- 2/3 of an hour = 40 minutes
- 5/6 of an hour = 50 minutes"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]Improper Fractions{note:"An improper fraction has a numerator greater than or equal to the denominator, so its value is 1 or greater. Examples: 5/3, 7/4, 8/8, 9/2. Improper fractions are useful in calculations because they avoid mixed number notation which can be cumbersome in multiplication and division. They represent quantities of 1 or more wholes plus a fractional part."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples of improper fractions and conversions to mixed numbers."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Identifying Improper Fractions{note:"Improper fractions have numerator ≥ denominator.

Examples:
- 3/2 (1½) - 3 > 2
- 5/3 (1⅔) - 5 > 3
- 7/4 (1¾) - 7 > 4
- 11/6 (1⅚) - 11 > 6
- 8/8 = 1 - 8 = 8
- 12/5 = 2⅖ - 12 > 5
- 9/4 = 2¼ - 9 > 4
- 23/8 = 2⅞ - 23 > 8

Improper fractions are greater than or equal to 1.
They can be written as mixed numbers."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Converting to Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert improper fractions to mixed numbers by dividing.

Method: Divide numerator by denominator.
- Quotient is the whole number part
- Remainder is the numerator of the fractional part
- Denominator stays the same

Examples:
- 7/3 = 2⅓ (3 goes into 7 twice, remainder 1)
- 11/4 = 2¾ (4 goes into 11 twice, remainder 3)
- 23/8 = 2⅞ (8 goes into 23 twice, remainder 7)
- 15/4 = 3¾ (4 goes into 15 three times, remainder 3)
- 14/5 = 2⅘ (5 goes into 14 two times, remainder 4)
- 18/5 = 3⅗ (5 goes into 18 three times, remainder 3)
- 27/4 = 6¾ (4 goes into 27 six times, remainder 3)
- 13/2 = 6½ (2 goes into 13 six times, remainder 1)

Check:
- 2 × 3 + 1 = 7 → 7/3 = 2⅓
- 2 × 4 + 3 = 11 → 11/4 = 2¾
- 2 × 8 + 7 = 23 → 23/8 = 2⅞"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Converting from Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert mixed numbers to improper fractions.

Method: (whole × denominator + numerator) / denominator

Examples:
- 1½ = 3/2 (1×2+1=3)
- 2¾ = 11/4 (2×4+3=11)
- 3⅕ = 16/5 (3×5+1=16)
- 4⅔ = 14/3 (4×3+2=14)
- 5¼ = 21/4 (5×4+1=21)
- 3⅜ = 27/8 (3×8+3=27)
- 2⅚ = 17/6 (2×6+5=17)
- 7½ = 15/2 (7×2+1=15)

Check:
- 1½ = 3/2 → 3 ÷ 2 = 1.5 ✓
- 2¾ = 11/4 → 11 ÷ 4 = 2.75 ✓
- 3⅕ = 16/5 → 16 ÷ 5 = 3.2 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Operations with Improper Fractions{note:"Improper fractions make multiplication and division easier.

Multiplication:
- 7/4 × 3/2 = 21/8 = 2⅝
- 5/3 × 6/5 = 30/15 = 2
- 9/2 × 4/3 = 36/6 = 6
- 11/6 × 3/4 = 33/24 = 11/8 = 1⅜

Division:
- 9/2 ÷ 3/4 = 9/2 × 4/3 = 36/6 = 6
- 7/3 ÷ 2/5 = 7/3 × 5/2 = 35/6 = 5⅚
- 11/4 ÷ 3/2 = 11/4 × 2/3 = 22/12 = 11/6 = 1⅚
- 13/5 ÷ 4/3 = 13/5 × 3/4 = 39/20 = 1 19/20

Addition/Subtraction (with common denominator):
- 7/4 + 3/2 = 7/4 + 6/4 = 13/4 = 3¼
- 9/2 - 5/3 = 27/6 - 10/6 = 17/6 = 2⅚
- 11/6 + 5/3 = 11/6 + 10/6 = 21/6 = 7/2 = 3½

Why use improper fractions?
- Avoids confusion in multiplication: 2½ × 1¾ = (5/2) × (7/4) = 35/8 = 4⅜
- Avoids confusion in division: 3½ ÷ 2¼ = (7/2) ÷ (9/4) = (7/2) × (4/9) = 28/18 = 14/9 = 1⅚"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]Mixed Numbers{note:"A mixed number combines a whole number and a proper fraction, representing a value greater than 1. Examples: 2½, 3¾, 1⅔. Mixed numbers are convenient for everyday use (recipes, measurements) because they clearly show the number of wholes and the fractional part. However, in calculations, converting to improper fractions is often easier."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples of mixed numbers and conversions to improper fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Understanding Mixed Numbers{note:"Mixed numbers show whole parts plus fractional parts.

Examples:
- 1½ (one and a half) = 1 whole + 1/2
- 2¾ (two and three-quarters) = 2 wholes + 3/4
- 3⅕ (three and one-fifth) = 3 wholes + 1/5
- 4⅔ (four and two-thirds) = 4 wholes + 2/3
- 5½ (five and a half) = 5 wholes + 1/2
- 6¼ (six and a quarter) = 6 wholes + 1/4
- 7⅜ (seven and three-eighths) = 7 wholes + 3/8
- 8⅚ (eight and five-sixths) = 8 wholes + 5/6

Visual representation:
2¾ = 2 whole circles + 3/4 of a circle
3⅕ = 3 whole squares + 1/5 of a square"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Converting to Improper Fractions{note:"Convert mixed numbers to improper fractions.

Formula: (whole × denominator + numerator) / denominator

Examples:
- 1½ = 3/2
  (1×2+1=3 → 3/2)
- 2¾ = 11/4
  (2×4+3=11 → 11/4)
- 3⅕ = 16/5
  (3×5+1=16 → 16/5)
- 4⅔ = 14/3
  (4×3+2=14 → 14/3)
- 5¼ = 21/4
  (5×4+1=21 → 21/4)
- 6½ = 13/2
  (6×2+1=13 → 13/2)
- 3⅜ = 27/8
  (3×8+3=27 → 27/8)
- 7⅚ = 47/6
  (7×6+5=47 → 47/6)

Check by converting back:
- 3/2 = 1½ ✓
- 11/4 = 2¾ ✓
- 16/5 = 3⅕ ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Operations with Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert to improper fractions for operations.

Addition:
- 1½ + 2¾ = 3/2 + 11/4 = 6/4 + 11/4 = 17/4 = 4¼
- 2⅓ + 3¼ = 7/3 + 13/4 = 28/12 + 39/12 = 67/12 = 5 7/12
- 3½ + 4¼ = 7/2 + 17/4 = 14/4 + 17/4 = 31/4 = 7¾

Subtraction:
- 4⅔ - 1½ = 14/3 - 3/2 = 28/6 - 9/6 = 19/6 = 3⅙
- 5¾ - 2¼ = 23/4 - 9/4 = 14/4 = 7/2 = 3½
- 7½ - 3⅓ = 15/2 - 10/3 = 45/6 - 20/6 = 25/6 = 4⅙

Multiplication:
- 1½ × 2¼ = 3/2 × 9/4 = 27/8 = 3⅜
- 2⅓ × 3½ = 7/3 × 7/2 = 49/6 = 8⅙
- 3¾ × 1⅕ = 15/4 × 6/5 = 90/20 = 9/2 = 4½

Division:
- 2⅓ ÷ 1½ = 7/3 ÷ 3/2 = 7/3 × 2/3 = 14/9 = 1⅚
- 3¾ ÷ 1¼ = 15/4 ÷ 5/4 = 15/4 × 4/5 = 60/20 = 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Real-World Applications{note:"Mixed numbers are common in everyday life.

Recipes:
- 2½ cups of flour
- 1¾ cups of sugar
- 3⅓ cups of milk
- 1½ teaspoons of baking powder
- 2¾ cups of chocolate chips

Measurements:
- 5⅜ inches (5 whole inches + 3/8 inch)
- 3¾ yards (3 yards + 3/4 yard)
- 2⅔ feet (2 feet + 8 inches)
- 1½ miles (1 mile + 1/2 mile)

Time:
- 1½ hours (1 hour 30 minutes)
- 2¼ hours (2 hours 15 minutes)
- 3¾ hours (3 hours 45 minutes)
- 4½ hours (4 hours 30 minutes)

Distance:
- 4⅓ miles
- 2¾ kilometers
- 5½ blocks

Weight:
- 3½ pounds
- 2¼ kilograms
- 1⅓ ounces"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]Equivalent Fractions{note:"Equivalent fractions are fractions that represent the same value but have different numerators and denominators. They are created by multiplying or dividing both numerator and denominator by the same non-zero number. Example: 1/2 = 2/4 = 3/6 = 4/8. Equivalent fractions are essential for comparing fractions, finding common denominators for addition/subtraction, and simplifying fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing equivalent fractions and how to find them."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Creating Equivalent Fractions{note:"Multiply or divide numerator and denominator by the same number.

Multiplying (making larger):
- 1/3 = 2/6 (×2), 3/9 (×3), 4/12 (×4), 5/15 (×5)
- 2/5 = 4/10 (×2), 6/15 (×3), 8/20 (×4), 10/25 (×5)
- 3/4 = 6/8 (×2), 9/12 (×3), 12/16 (×4), 15/20 (×5)
- 1/2 = 2/4 = 3/6 = 4/8 = 5/10 = 6/12 = 7/14
- 2/3 = 4/6 = 6/9 = 8/12 = 10/15 = 12/18

Dividing (simplifying):
- 4/6 = 2/3 (÷2)
- 9/12 = 3/4 (÷3)
- 8/10 = 4/5 (÷2)
- 12/16 = 3/4 (÷4)
- 10/15 = 2/3 (÷5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Identifying Equivalent Fractions{note:"Equivalent fractions can be identified by cross multiplication.

Examples:
- 2/3 = 4/6 → 2×6 = 12, 3×4 = 12 (equal)
- 2/3 = 6/9 → 2×9 = 18, 3×6 = 18 (equal)
- 2/3 = 8/12 → 2×12 = 24, 3×8 = 24 (equal)
- 3/4 = 6/8 → 3×8 = 24, 4×6 = 24 (equal)
- 3/4 = 9/12 → 3×12 = 36, 4×9 = 36 (equal)
- 5/8 = 10/16 → 5×16 = 80, 8×10 = 80 (equal)
- 5/8 = 15/24 → 5×24 = 120, 8×15 = 120 (equal)

Not equivalent:
- 1/2 and 3/4: 1×4=4, 2×3=6 → not equal
- 2/3 and 3/4: 2×4=8, 3×3=9 → not equal
- 3/5 and 4/7: 3×7=21, 5×4=20 → not equal"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Finding Common Denominators{note:"Equivalent fractions are used to find common denominators.

Finding LCD (least common denominator):
- For 1/2 and 1/3, LCD = 6
  1/2 = 3/6, 1/3 = 2/6

- For 2/3 and 3/4, LCD = 12
  2/3 = 8/12, 3/4 = 9/12

- For 5/6 and 3/8, LCD = 24
  5/6 = 20/24, 3/8 = 9/24

- For 7/10 and 4/15, LCD = 30
  7/10 = 21/30, 4/15 = 8/30

- For 3/5 and 7/10, LCD = 10
  3/5 = 6/10, 7/10 = 7/10

Why find common denominators?
- To add or subtract fractions
- To compare fractions
- To solve equations with fractions"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Simplifying Fractions{note:"Simplifying reduces fractions to lowest terms.

Method: Divide numerator and denominator by their GCF.

Examples:
- 12/18 → GCF=6 → 2/3
- 15/20 → GCF=5 → 3/4
- 24/36 → GCF=12 → 2/3
- 48/72 → GCF=24 → 2/3
- 100/150 → GCF=50 → 2/3
- 63/81 → GCF=9 → 7/9
- 72/120 → GCF=24 → 3/5
- 84/96 → GCF=12 → 7/8
- 45/60 → GCF=15 → 3/4
- 30/45 → GCF=15 → 2/3

Mixed numbers:
- 2 12/18 = 2 2/3 (simplify fraction part)
- 3 15/20 = 3 3/4
- 5 24/36 = 5 2/3
- 4 16/24 = 4 2/3

A fraction is in simplest form when numerator and denominator have no common factors other than 1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]Simplifying Fractions{note:"Simplifying a fraction (also called reducing) means expressing it in its lowest terms by dividing both numerator and denominator by their greatest common factor (GCF). Example: 12/18 = 2/3 (divide by 6). Simplified fractions are easier to work with and understand. A fraction is in simplest form when the numerator and denominator have no common factors other than 1."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing how to simplify fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Simplification{note:"Simplify by dividing by the GCF.

Examples:
- 12/18 → divide by 6 (GCF = 6) → 2/3
- 15/20 → divide by 5 (GCF = 5) → 3/4
- 24/36 → divide by 12 (GCF = 12) → 2/3
- 16/24 → divide by 8 (GCF = 8) → 2/3
- 18/27 → divide by 9 (GCF = 9) → 2/3
- 10/25 → divide by 5 (GCF = 5) → 2/5
- 14/21 → divide by 7 (GCF = 7) → 2/3
- 20/30 → divide by 10 (GCF = 10) → 2/3

Check: 2/3 × 6 = 12/18 ✓
       3/4 × 5 = 15/20 ✓
       2/3 × 12 = 24/36 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Simplifying Larger Fractions{note:"Larger fractions can be simplified step by step.

Examples:
- 48/72 → divide by 24 (GCF=24) → 2/3
  48÷24=2, 72÷24=3
- 100/150 → divide by 50 (GCF=50) → 2/3
  100÷50=2, 150÷50=3
- 63/81 → divide by 9 (GCF=9) → 7/9
  63÷9=7, 81÷9=9
- 72/120 → divide by 24 (GCF=24) → 3/5
  72÷24=3, 120÷24=5
- 84/96 → divide by 12 (GCF=12) → 7/8
  84÷12=7, 96÷12=8
- 126/180 → divide by 18 (GCF=18) → 7/10
  126÷18=7, 180÷18=10
- 150/200 → divide by 50 (GCF=50) → 3/4
  150÷50=3, 200÷50=4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Simplifying Mixed Numbers{note:"Simplify the fractional part of mixed numbers.

Examples:
- 2 12/18 = 2 2/3 (12/18 simplifies to 2/3)
  12÷6=2, 18÷6=3
- 3 15/20 = 3 3/4 (15/20 simplifies to 3/4)
  15÷5=3, 20÷5=4
- 5 24/36 = 5 2/3 (24/36 simplifies to 2/3)
  24÷12=2, 36÷12=3
- 4 16/24 = 4 2/3 (16/24 simplifies to 2/3)
  16÷8=2, 24÷8=3
- 6 18/27 = 6 2/3 (18/27 simplifies to 2/3)
  18÷9=2, 27÷9=3
- 7 25/35 = 7 5/7 (25/35 simplifies to 5/7)
  25÷5=5, 35÷5=7

The whole number part stays the same; only the fraction is simplified."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Simplifying Improper Fractions{note:"Simplify improper fractions and convert to mixed numbers.

Examples:
- 12/8 = 12÷4/8÷4 = 3/2 = 1½
- 18/12 = 18÷6/12÷6 = 3/2 = 1½
- 50/30 = 50÷10/30÷10 = 5/3 = 1⅔
- 100/80 = 100÷20/80÷20 = 5/4 = 1¼
- 24/16 = 24÷8/16÷8 = 3/2 = 1½
- 45/30 = 45÷15/30÷15 = 3/2 = 1½
- 42/28 = 42÷14/28÷14 = 3/2 = 1½

Step-by-step:
- 12/8 → divide by 4 → 3/2 → divide numerator by denominator → 1 remainder 1 → 1½
- 50/30 → divide by 10 → 5/3 → 1 remainder 2 → 1⅔"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Finding the GCF{note:"The GCF is needed to simplify fractions.

Finding GCF by listing factors:
- 12 and 18:
  Factors of 12: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12
  Factors of 18: 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 18
  Common factors: 1, 2, 3, 6
  GCF = 6

- 24 and 36:
  Factors of 24: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24
  Factors of 36: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36
  Common factors: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12
  GCF = 12

Finding GCF by prime factorization:
- 48 = 2⁴ × 3, 72 = 2³ × 3²
  GCF = 2³ × 3 = 8×3 = 24

- 84 = 2² × 3 × 7, 96 = 2⁵ × 3
  GCF = 2² × 3 = 4×3 = 12

- 100 = 2² × 5², 150 = 2 × 3 × 5²
  GCF = 2 × 5² = 2×25 = 50"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]Decimal Place Values{note:"Decimal place values extend the place value system to the right of the decimal point. Each position represents a fraction of 10: tenths (1/10), hundredths (1/100), thousandths (1/1000), etc. Example: 3.456 means 3 ones, 4 tenths, 5 hundredths, 6 thousandths. Understanding decimal place values is essential for comparing decimals, performing operations, and making precise measurements."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing decimal place values and how to read them."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Understanding Place Values{note:"Each position after the decimal represents a fraction of 10.

Place values:
- 0.1 = 1/10 (tenths)
- 0.01 = 1/100 (hundredths)
- 0.001 = 1/1000 (thousandths)
- 0.0001 = 1/10000 (ten-thousandths)
- 0.00001 = 1/100000 (hundred-thousandths)
- 0.000001 = 1/1000000 (millionths)

Examples:
- 3.456 = 3 + 4/10 + 5/100 + 6/1000
  3 ones, 4 tenths, 5 hundredths, 6 thousandths
- 12.34 = 12 + 3/10 + 4/100
  12 ones, 3 tenths, 4 hundredths
- 5.678 = 5 + 6/10 + 7/100 + 8/1000
  5 ones, 6 tenths, 7 hundredths, 8 thousandths
- 0.025 = 0 + 0/10 + 2/100 + 5/1000
  2 hundredths, 5 thousandths"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Reading Decimals{note:"Read decimals by naming the place value of the last digit.

Examples:
- 0.7 = seven tenths
- 0.25 = twenty-five hundredths
- 0.125 = one hundred twenty-five thousandths
- 2.34 = two and thirty-four hundredths
- 5.006 = five and six thousandths
- 10.05 = ten and five hundredths
- 0.0005 = five ten-thousandths
- 3.1415 = three and one thousand four hundred fifteen ten-thousandths

Whole number part + "and" + fractional part:
- 3.456 = three and four hundred fifty-six thousandths
- 12.34 = twelve and thirty-four hundredths
- 5.067 = five and sixty-seven thousandths
- 0.123 = one hundred twenty-three thousandths"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Comparing Decimals{note:"Compare decimals by looking at place values from left to right.

Examples:
- 0.3 > 0.25 (3/10 > 25/100)
  0.3 = 0.30, 0.30 > 0.25
- 0.75 > 0.7 (75/100 > 70/100)
  0.75 > 0.70
- 0.08 < 0.1 (8/100 < 10/100)
  0.08 < 0.10
- 0.333... > 0.33 (1/3 > 33/100)
  0.333... > 0.330
- 0.5 = 0.50 = 0.500 (zeros after the last digit don't change value)
- 1.05 < 1.10 (compare hundredths: 5 < 10)

Rules for comparison:
1. Compare whole number parts first
2. If equal, compare tenths
3. If equal, compare hundredths
4. Continue to the right as needed"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Expanded Notation{note:"Decimals can be written in expanded notation.

Examples:
- 3.456 = 3×1 + 4×0.1 + 5×0.01 + 6×0.001
  = 3 + 0.4 + 0.05 + 0.006
- 12.34 = 1×10 + 2×1 + 3×0.1 + 4×0.01
  = 10 + 2 + 0.3 + 0.04
- 5.678 = 5×1 + 6×0.1 + 7×0.01 + 8×0.001
  = 5 + 0.6 + 0.07 + 0.008
- 0.025 = 0×1 + 0×0.1 + 2×0.01 + 5×0.001
  = 0.02 + 0.005
- 34.567 = 30 + 4 + 0.5 + 0.06 + 0.007

Fraction form:
- 3.456 = 3 456/1000
- 12.34 = 12 34/100
- 5.678 = 5 678/1000"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Rounding Decimals{note:"Rounding decimals to a given place value.

Rules:
- Look at the digit to the right of the place value
- If digit ≥ 5, round up (increase the digit by 1)
- If digit < 5, round down (keep the digit the same)

Examples:
- Round 3.456 to nearest tenth:
  Look at hundredths (5): 5 ≥ 5 → round up
  3.456 → 3.5

- Round 3.456 to nearest hundredth:
  Look at thousandths (6): 6 ≥ 5 → round up
  3.456 → 3.46

- Round 12.34 to nearest whole:
  Look at tenths (3): 3 < 5 → round down
  12.34 → 12

- Round 12.67 to nearest whole:
  Look at tenths (6): 6 ≥ 5 → round up
  12.67 → 13

- Round 5.678 to nearest hundredth:
  Look at thousandths (8): 8 ≥ 5 → round up
  5.678 → 5.68

- Round 5.674 to nearest hundredth:
  Look at thousandths (4): 4 < 5 → round down
  5.674 → 5.67

- Round 3.14159 to nearest thousandth:
  Look at ten-thousandths (5): 5 ≥ 5 → round up
  3.14159 → 3.142"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]Converting Fractions to Decimals{note:"To convert a fraction to a decimal, divide the numerator by the denominator. The result can be a terminating decimal (ending) or a repeating decimal (with a repeating pattern of digits). This conversion is essential for comparing fractions, using calculators, and applying mathematics in science and engineering where decimals are the standard."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing fraction to decimal conversions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Terminating Decimals{note:"Divide numerator by denominator to get terminating decimals.

Examples:
- 1/2 = 0.5
- 1/4 = 0.25
- 3/4 = 0.75
- 1/5 = 0.2
- 2/5 = 0.4
- 3/5 = 0.6
- 4/5 = 0.8
- 1/8 = 0.125
- 3/8 = 0.375
- 5/8 = 0.625
- 7/8 = 0.875
- 1/10 = 0.1
- 1/16 = 0.0625
- 1/20 = 0.05
- 1/25 = 0.04
- 1/40 = 0.025

Long division:
- 3/8 = 3 ÷ 8 = 0.375
  8 goes into 3 zero times → 0.
  Bring down decimal: 8 goes into 30 three times → 0.3, remainder 6
  8 goes into 60 seven times → 0.37, remainder 4
  8 goes into 40 five times → 0.375"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Repeating Decimals{note:"Divide to get repeating decimals.

Examples:
- 1/3 = 0.333... (0.3 with bar over 3)
- 2/3 = 0.666... (0.6 with bar over 6)
- 1/6 = 0.1666... (0.16 with bar over 6)
- 5/6 = 0.8333... (0.83 with bar over 3)
- 1/7 = 0.142857142857... (0.142857 with bar)
- 2/7 = 0.285714285714... (0.285714 with bar)
- 1/9 = 0.111... (0.1 with bar)
- 1/11 = 0.090909... (0.09 with bar)
- 1/12 = 0.08333... (0.083 with bar)
- 1/13 = 0.076923076923... (0.076923 with bar)

Long division:
- 1/3 = 1 ÷ 3 = 0.333...
  3 goes into 1 zero times → 0.
  Bring down decimal: 3 goes into 10 three times → 0.3, remainder 1
  3 goes into 10 three times → 0.33, remainder 1
  Repeats indefinitely → 0.333..."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert mixed numbers to decimals.

Examples:
- 1½ = 1.5 (whole 1 + 0.5)
  1/2 = 0.5 → 1.5
- 2¾ = 2.75 (whole 2 + 0.75)
  3/4 = 0.75 → 2.75
- 3⅕ = 3.2 (whole 3 + 0.2)
  1/5 = 0.2 → 3.2
- 4⅔ = 4.666... (whole 4 + 0.666...)
  2/3 = 0.666... → 4.666...
- 5¼ = 5.25 (whole 5 + 0.25)
  1/4 = 0.25 → 5.25
- 3⅜ = 3.375 (whole 3 + 0.375)
  3/8 = 0.375 → 3.375

Method:
1. Convert fraction part to decimal
2. Add to whole number part"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]When Do Decimals Terminate?{note:"A fraction terminates if the denominator (in simplest form) has only prime factors 2 and 5.

Terminating:
- 1/2 (denominator 2) → terminates
- 3/4 (denominator 4 = 2²) → terminates
- 7/8 (denominator 8 = 2³) → terminates
- 1/5 (denominator 5) → terminates
- 1/10 (denominator 10 = 2×5) → terminates
- 1/25 (denominator 25 = 5²) → terminates
- 1/40 (denominator 40 = 2³×5) → terminates
- 1/16 (denominator 16 = 2⁴) → terminates

Repeating:
- 1/3 (denominator 3) → repeats
- 1/6 (denominator 6 = 2×3) → repeats
- 1/7 (denominator 7) → repeats
- 1/9 (denominator 9 = 3²) → repeats
- 1/11 (denominator 11) → repeats
- 1/12 (denominator 12 = 2²×3) → repeats
- 1/13 (denominator 13) → repeats
- 1/15 (denominator 15 = 3×5) → repeats"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]Converting Decimals to Fractions{note:"To convert a decimal to a fraction, determine the place value of the last digit and use that as the denominator, simplifying if possible. For repeating decimals, use algebraic methods to find the fraction. This conversion is useful for exact calculations, understanding proportions, and when fractions are preferred in mathematical contexts."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing decimal to fraction conversions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Terminating Decimals to Fractions{note:"Write the decimal as a fraction with denominator as a power of 10.

Examples:
- 0.5 = 5/10 = 1/2
- 0.75 = 75/100 = 3/4
- 0.125 = 125/1000 = 1/8
- 0.625 = 625/1000 = 5/8
- 0.875 = 875/1000 = 7/8
- 0.25 = 1/4
- 0.2 = 1/5
- 0.4 = 2/5
- 0.6 = 3/5
- 0.8 = 4/5
- 0.05 = 1/20
- 0.025 = 1/40
- 1.25 = 125/100 = 5/4 = 1¼
- 2.5 = 25/10 = 5/2 = 2½

Method:
1. Determine place value of last digit
2. Write as fraction (numerator = digits, denominator = place value)
3. Simplify"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Repeating Decimals to Fractions{note:"Use algebra to convert repeating decimals to fractions.

Single repeating digit:
- 0.333... = 1/3
- 0.666... = 2/3
- 0.777... = 7/9
- 0.111... = 1/9
- 0.999... = 1

Algebraic method for 0.777...:
  Let x = 0.777...
  10x = 7.777...
  Subtract: 10x - x = 7.777... - 0.777...
  9x = 7
  x = 7/9

Multiple repeating digits:
- 0.454545... = 5/11
- 0.121212... = 4/33
- 0.142857... = 1/7
- 0.090909... = 1/11
- 0.363636... = 4/11

Algebraic method for 0.454545...:
  Let x = 0.454545...
  100x = 45.454545...
  Subtract: 100x - x = 45.454545... - 0.454545...
  99x = 45
  x = 45/99 = 5/11"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Mixed Repeating Decimals{note:"Decimals with non-repeating and repeating parts.

Examples:
- 0.1666... = (16-1)/90 = 15/90 = 1/6
- 0.8333... = (83-8)/90 = 75/90 = 5/6
- 0.38333... = (383-38)/900 = 345/900 = 23/60
- 0.51666... = (516-51)/900 = 465/900 = 31/60
- 0.12333... = (123-12)/900 = 111/900 = 37/300

Method:
For 0.38333...
  Let x = 0.38333...
  100x = 38.333...
  1000x = 383.333...
  Subtract: 1000x - 100x = 383.333... - 38.333...
  900x = 345
  x = 345/900 = 23/60

For 0.51666...
  100x = 51.666...
  1000x = 516.666...
  900x = 465
  x = 465/900 = 31/60"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Special Case: 0.999...{note:"0.999... = 1 (exactly, not approximately).

Proof 1:
  Let x = 0.999...
  10x = 9.999...
  Subtract: 10x - x = 9.999... - 0.999... = 9
  9x = 9
  x = 1

Proof 2:
  0.999... = 9/10 + 9/100 + 9/1000 + ...
  = 9(1/10 + 1/100 + 1/1000 + ...)
  = 9 × (1/9) = 1

Proof 3:
  1/3 = 0.333...
  3 × (1/3) = 3 × 0.333... = 0.999...
  1 = 0.999...

This is a well-known mathematical fact that demonstrates the nature of infinite decimals."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Fraction Equivalents to Memorize{note:"Common decimal to fraction equivalents.

Decimals to fractions:
0.1 = 1/10
0.2 = 1/5
0.25 = 1/4
0.3 = 3/10
0.333... = 1/3
0.4 = 2/5
0.5 = 1/2
0.6 = 3/5
0.666... = 2/3
0.7 = 7/10
0.75 = 3/4
0.8 = 4/5
0.833... = 5/6
0.875 = 7/8
0.9 = 9/10

Percent equivalents:
0.01 = 1/100 = 1%
0.1 = 1/10 = 10%
0.25 = 1/4 = 25%
0.333... = 1/3 = 33⅓%
0.5 = 1/2 = 50%
0.666... = 2/3 = 66⅔%
0.75 = 3/4 = 75%
1 = 1 = 100%

These are useful for quick mental calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]Repeating Decimals{note:"Repeating decimals (also called recurring decimals) have one or more digits that repeat infinitely. They are always rational numbers (can be expressed as fractions). Examples: 0.333..., 0.142857142857..., 0.090909... The repeating block is often written with a bar: 0.¯3, 0.¯142857, 0.¯09. Recognizing repeating decimals is important for converting them to exact fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples of repeating decimals and their fraction equivalents."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Single Repeating Digit{note:"One digit repeats indefinitely.

Fractions:
- 0.111... = 1/9
- 0.222... = 2/9
- 0.333... = 3/9 = 1/3
- 0.444... = 4/9
- 0.555... = 5/9
- 0.666... = 6/9 = 2/3
- 0.777... = 7/9
- 0.888... = 8/9
- 0.999... = 9/9 = 1

Pattern: 0.ddd... = d/9 where d is the repeating digit.

Examples:
- 0.333... = 3/9 = 1/3
- 0.666... = 6/9 = 2/3
- 0.999... = 9/9 = 1

Notation:
- 0.333... = 0.3̅ (bar over 3)
- 0.777... = 0.7̅ (bar over 7)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Multiple Repeating Digits{note:"Two or more digits repeat in a block.

Fractions:
- 0.121212... = 12/99 = 4/33
- 0.232323... = 23/99
- 0.454545... = 45/99 = 5/11
- 0.909090... = 90/99 = 10/11
- 0.142857142857... = 142857/999999 = 1/7
- 0.090909... = 9/99 = 1/11
- 0.123123... = 123/999 = 41/333
- 0.012012... = 12/999 = 4/333
- 0.000111... = 111/999000 = 37/333000

Pattern: 0.abcabc... = abc/999 (for 3-digit block)
0.abab... = ab/99 (for 2-digit block)

Examples:
- 0.121212... = 12/99 = 4/33
- 0.123123... = 123/999 = 41/333
- 0.142857... = 142857/999999 = 1/7"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Notation for Repeating Decimals{note:"Use a bar over the repeating block.

Examples:
- 0.333... = 0.3̅
- 0.666... = 0.6̅
- 0.142857142857... = 0.142857̅
- 0.090909... = 0.09̅
- 0.1666... = 0.16̅ (bar over 6 only)
- 0.8333... = 0.83̅ (bar over 3 only)
- 0.123123... = 0.123̅
- 0.454545... = 0.45̅
- 0.333... = 0.3̅

Bar placement:
- Single digit: bar over the digit
- Multiple digits: bar over the repeating block
- Mixed: bar over the repeating part only

Examples:
- 0.16̅ means 0.1666...
- 0.83̅ means 0.8333...
- 0.142857̅ means 0.142857142857..."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Converting Repeating Decimals to Fractions{note:"Algebraic method works for all repeating decimals.

Example 1: 0.777...
  x = 0.777...
  10x = 7.777...
  10x - x = 7.777... - 0.777...
  9x = 7
  x = 7/9

Example 2: 0.454545...
  x = 0.454545...
  100x = 45.454545...
  100x - x = 45.454545... - 0.454545...
  99x = 45
  x = 45/99 = 5/11

Example 3: 0.123123...
  x = 0.123123...
  1000x = 123.123123...
  1000x - x = 123.123123... - 0.123123...
  999x = 123
  x = 123/999 = 41/333

Example 4: 0.1666...
  x = 0.1666...
  100x = 16.666...
  10x = 1.666...
  100x - 10x = 16.666... - 1.666...
  90x = 15
  x = 15/90 = 1/6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Special Cases in Repeating Decimals{note:"Some repeating decimals have special properties.

0.999... = 1 (exact equality):
- Many people are surprised by this fact
- It is not an approximation
- It follows from the algebraic method

0.000111... = 1/9000:
  x = 0.000111...
  10000x = 1.111...
  1000x = 0.111...
  9000x = 1
  x = 1/9000

0.12333... = 0.123̅:
  x = 0.12333...
  1000x = 123.333...
  100x = 12.333...
  900x = 111
  x = 111/900 = 37/300

0.123123... = 0.123̅:
  x = 0.123123...
  1000x = 123.123...
  999x = 123
  x = 41/333

Recognizing the pattern helps:
- 0.123123... has a 3-digit repeating block
- 0.12333... has a 2-digit non-repeating part before the repeating digit"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]Terminating Decimals{note:"Terminating decimals have a finite number of digits after the decimal point. They are rational numbers that can be written with a denominator that is a power of 10 (after simplification, the denominator only has factors of 2 and 5). Examples: 0.5, 0.75, 0.125, 0.625. Terminating decimals are exact and easy to work with in calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples of terminating decimals and their fraction equivalents."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]One Decimal Place{note:"Decimals with one digit after the decimal point.

Fractions:
- 0.1 = 1/10
- 0.2 = 1/5
- 0.3 = 3/10
- 0.4 = 2/5
- 0.5 = 1/2
- 0.6 = 3/5
- 0.7 = 7/10
- 0.8 = 4/5
- 0.9 = 9/10
- 1.0 = 1

All are terminating because denominators are 10 or simplified factors.

Examples:
- 0.5 = 5/10 = 1/2
- 0.8 = 8/10 = 4/5
- 0.3 = 3/10

These are the most common terminating decimals."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Two Decimal Places{note:"Decimals with two digits after the decimal point.

Fractions:
- 0.25 = 1/4
- 0.50 = 1/2
- 0.75 = 3/4
- 0.40 = 2/5
- 0.60 = 3/5
- 0.80 = 4/5
- 0.20 = 1/5
- 0.30 = 3/10
- 0.70 = 7/10
- 0.90 = 9/10

All are terminating because denominators are 100 or simplified factors.

Examples:
- 0.25 = 25/100 = 1/4
- 0.75 = 75/100 = 3/4
- 0.40 = 40/100 = 2/5

These are common in money (cents) and percentages."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Three Decimal Places{note:"Decimals with three digits after the decimal point.

Fractions:
- 0.125 = 1/8
- 0.250 = 1/4
- 0.375 = 3/8
- 0.500 = 1/2
- 0.625 = 5/8
- 0.875 = 7/8
- 0.100 = 1/10
- 0.200 = 1/5
- 0.400 = 2/5
- 0.600 = 3/5

All are terminating because denominators are 1000 or simplified factors.

Examples:
- 0.125 = 125/1000 = 1/8
- 0.375 = 375/1000 = 3/8
- 0.625 = 625/1000 = 5/8

Common in measurements (inches to fractions of an inch)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Terminating vs Repeating{note:"Identify whether a fraction terminates or repeats.

Terminating:
- 3/8 = 0.375 (denominator 8 = 2³)
- 5/16 = 0.3125 (denominator 16 = 2⁴)
- 7/20 = 0.35 (denominator 20 = 2²×5)
- 9/25 = 0.36 (denominator 25 = 5²)
- 3/4 = 0.75 (denominator 4 = 2²)

Repeating:
- 3/7 = 0.428571... (denominator 7)
- 2/3 = 0.666... (denominator 3)
- 3/11 = 0.2727... (denominator 11)
- 5/6 = 0.833... (denominator 6 = 2×3)
- 7/9 = 0.777... (denominator 9 = 3²)

Rule: Terminating if denominator (simplified) has only prime factors 2 and 5.
Otherwise, repeating."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Condition for Terminating{note:"A fraction in simplest form terminates if and only if the denominator has no prime factors other than 2 and 5.

Terminating:
- 1/4 (4 = 2²) → terminates
- 1/5 (5) → terminates
- 1/8 (8 = 2³) → terminates
- 1/10 (10 = 2×5) → terminates
- 1/25 (25 = 5²) → terminates
- 1/40 (40 = 2³×5) → terminates
- 1/16 (16 = 2⁴) → terminates
- 1/50 (50 = 2×5²) → terminates

Repeating:
- 1/3 (3) → repeats
- 1/6 (6 = 2×3) → repeats
- 1/7 (7) → repeats
- 1/9 (9 = 3²) → repeats
- 1/11 (11) → repeats
- 1/12 (12 = 2²×3) → repeats
- 1/13 (13) → repeats
- 1/14 (14 = 2×7) → repeats

Check: 7/20: denominator 20 = 2²×5 → terminates
      5/6: denominator 6 = 2×3 → repeats"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]Adding and Subtracting Fractions{note:"To add or subtract fractions, they must have a common denominator. Find the least common denominator (LCD), convert each fraction to an equivalent fraction with the LCD, then add or subtract the numerators. For mixed numbers, convert to improper fractions or work with whole numbers separately. This is essential for combining fractional quantities in recipes, measurements, and many calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing addition and subtraction of fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Same Denominator{note:"Add/subtract numerators, keep denominator.

Addition:
- 3/8 + 2/8 = 5/8
- 5/12 + 7/12 = 12/12 = 1
- 2/5 + 1/5 = 3/5
- 4/9 + 3/9 = 7/9
- 3/10 + 5/10 = 8/10 = 4/5

Subtraction:
- 7/10 - 3/10 = 4/10 = 2/5
- 9/16 - 5/16 = 4/16 = 1/4
- 6/7 - 2/7 = 4/7
- 8/11 - 3/11 = 5/11
- 5/8 - 1/8 = 4/8 = 1/2

Simplify the result when possible:
- 8/10 = 4/5
- 4/16 = 1/4
- 4/8 = 1/2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Different Denominators{note:"Find LCD, convert, then add/subtract.

Addition:
- 1/2 + 1/3 = 3/6 + 2/6 = 5/6
- 3/4 + 2/3 = 9/12 + 8/12 = 17/12 = 1 5/12
- 3/5 + 1/4 = 12/20 + 5/20 = 17/20
- 2/3 + 1/6 = 4/6 + 1/6 = 5/6
- 5/8 + 3/4 = 5/8 + 6/8 = 11/8 = 1 3/8

Subtraction:
- 5/8 - 1/4 = 5/8 - 2/8 = 3/8
- 2/3 - 1/2 = 4/6 - 3/6 = 1/6
- 7/10 - 2/5 = 7/10 - 4/10 = 3/10
- 3/4 - 1/3 = 9/12 - 4/12 = 5/12
- 5/6 - 3/8 = 20/24 - 9/24 = 11/24

Finding LCD:
- For 1/2 and 1/3, LCD = 6
- For 3/4 and 2/3, LCD = 12
- For 5/6 and 3/8, LCD = 24
- For 7/10 and 4/15, LCD = 30
- For 3/5 and 7/10, LCD = 10"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert to improper fractions or add whole numbers separately.

Converting to improper fractions:
- 1½ + 2¾ = 3/2 + 11/4 = 6/4 + 11/4 = 17/4 = 4¼
- 3⅓ - 1½ = 10/3 - 3/2 = 20/6 - 9/6 = 11/6 = 1⅚
- 2¼ + 3½ = 9/4 + 7/2 = 9/4 + 14/4 = 23/4 = 5¾
- 4⅔ - 1¼ = 14/3 - 5/4 = 56/12 - 15/12 = 41/12 = 3 5/12
- 5½ + 2¾ = 11/2 + 11/4 = 22/4 + 11/4 = 33/4 = 8¼

Adding whole and fraction separately:
- 3½ + 2¼ = (3+2) + (1/2+1/4) = 5 + (2/4+1/4) = 5 + 3/4 = 5¾
- 5⅔ - 2¼ = (5-2) + (2/3-1/4) = 3 + (8/12-3/12) = 3 + 5/12 = 3 5/12

Borrowing in subtraction:
- 3⅓ - 1½ = 3⅓ - 1½ = 3 2/6 - 1 3/6 = (2 8/6) - 1 3/6 = 1 5/6"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Adding Three Fractions{note:"Find the LCD and add all numerators.

Examples:
- 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/4 = 6/12 + 4/12 + 3/12 = 13/12 = 1 1/12
- 2/3 + 3/5 + 1/10 = 20/30 + 18/30 + 3/30 = 41/30 = 1 11/30
- 5/6 + 2/3 - 1/2 = 5/6 + 4/6 - 3/6 = 6/6 = 1
- 3/4 + 1/2 + 5/8 = 6/8 + 4/8 + 5/8 = 15/8 = 1 7/8
- 7/10 + 3/5 - 1/2 = 7/10 + 6/10 - 5/10 = 8/10 = 4/5

Mixed numbers:
- 1½ + 2¼ + 3¾ = 3/2 + 9/4 + 15/4 = 6/4 + 9/4 + 15/4 = 30/4 = 7½
- 2⅓ + 3⅚ - 1½ = 7/3 + 23/6 - 3/2 = 14/6 + 23/6 - 9/6 = 28/6 = 14/3 = 4⅔

When adding three or more fractions, find the least common denominator of all denominators."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Applications of Fraction Addition/Subtraction{note:"Real-world applications of adding and subtracting fractions.

Recipes:
- Recipe requires 2/3 cup flour, 1/4 cup sugar, 1/2 cup milk
  Total: 2/3 + 1/4 + 1/2 = 8/12 + 3/12 + 6/12 = 17/12 = 1 5/12 cups
- If you have 1 1/2 cups of flour and need 2 1/4 cups, how much more?
  2¼ - 1½ = 9/4 - 3/2 = 9/4 - 6/4 = 3/4 cup

Distance:
- Walk 3/4 mile, then 1/2 mile, then 5/8 mile
  Total: 3/4 + 1/2 + 5/8 = 6/8 + 4/8 + 5/8 = 15/8 = 1 7/8 miles
- If total distance is 5 miles and you've walked 2 3/4 miles, remaining = 5 - 2¾ = 5 - 11/4 = 20/4 - 11/4 = 9/4 = 2¼ miles

Time:
- 1½ hours + 2¾ hours + 1¼ hours = 1½ + 2¾ + 1¼ = 5½ hours
- If you spend 3/4 hour on math and 2/3 hour on science, total = 3/4 + 2/3 = 9/12 + 8/12 = 17/12 = 1 5/12 hours"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]Multiplying and Dividing Fractions{note:"To multiply fractions, multiply the numerators together and the denominators together. Simplify if possible. To divide fractions, multiply by the reciprocal of the divisor (flip the second fraction). For mixed numbers, convert to improper fractions first. These operations are used in scaling recipes, finding fractions of quantities, and many proportional calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing multiplication and division of fractions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Multiplying Fractions{note:"Multiply numerators and denominators, then simplify.

Examples:
- 2/3 × 3/4 = 6/12 = 1/2
- 5/6 × 3/5 = 15/30 = 1/2
- 7/8 × 4/21 = 28/168 = 1/6
- 3/4 × 8/9 = 24/36 = 2/3
- 2/5 × 5/8 = 10/40 = 1/4
- 1/2 × 2/3 = 2/6 = 1/3
- 4/7 × 7/12 = 28/84 = 1/3
- 3/10 × 5/9 = 15/90 = 1/6

Cross-cancellation (simplify before multiplying):
- 2/3 × 9/4 = (2×9)/(3×4) = (1×3)/(1×2) = 3/2
  (cancel 2 and 4, cancel 3 and 9)
- 5/6 × 12/25 = (5×12)/(6×25) = (1×2)/(1×5) = 2/5
  (cancel 5 and 25, cancel 6 and 12)
- 4/7 × 21/16 = (4×21)/(7×16) = (1×3)/(1×4) = 3/4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Multiplying Whole Numbers and Fractions{note:"Multiply the whole number by the numerator, keep denominator.

Examples:
- 6 × 3/4 = 18/4 = 9/2 = 4½
- 5 × 2/3 = 10/3 = 3⅓
- 8 × 3/8 = 24/8 = 3
- 10 × 3/5 = 30/5 = 6
- 4 × 5/6 = 20/6 = 10/3 = 3⅓
- 12 × 2/3 = 24/3 = 8
- 3 × 7/8 = 21/8 = 2⅝

Method: 6 × 3/4 = (6×3)/4 = 18/4 = 9/2 = 4½

Cross-cancellation:
- 6 × 3/4 = (6×3)/4 = (3×3)/2 = 9/2
  (cancel 6 and 4 by dividing by 2)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Multiplying Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert to improper fractions first.

Examples:
- 1½ × 2¼ = 3/2 × 9/4 = 27/8 = 3⅜
- 2⅓ × 3½ = 7/3 × 7/2 = 49/6 = 8⅙
- 3¾ × 1⅕ = 15/4 × 6/5 = 90/20 = 9/2 = 4½
- 2½ × 1¼ = 5/2 × 5/4 = 25/8 = 3⅛
- 4⅔ × 1½ = 14/3 × 3/2 = 42/6 = 7
- 3⅕ × 2¾ = 16/5 × 11/4 = 176/20 = 44/5 = 8⅘

Step-by-step:
1. Convert mixed numbers to improper fractions
2. Multiply numerators and denominators
3. Simplify the result
4. Convert back to mixed number if needed"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Dividing Fractions{note:"Multiply by the reciprocal of the divisor.

Examples:
- 3/4 ÷ 1/2 = 3/4 × 2/1 = 6/4 = 3/2 = 1½
- 5/6 ÷ 5/12 = 5/6 × 12/5 = 60/30 = 2
- 2/3 ÷ 4/5 = 2/3 × 5/4 = 10/12 = 5/6
- 7/8 ÷ 3/4 = 7/8 × 4/3 = 28/24 = 7/6 = 1⅙
- 3/5 ÷ 2/3 = 3/5 × 3/2 = 9/10
- 1/2 ÷ 1/4 = 1/2 × 4/1 = 4/2 = 2
- 5/6 ÷ 5/8 = 5/6 × 8/5 = 40/30 = 4/3 = 1⅓

Rule: "Keep, Change, Flip"
- Keep the first fraction
- Change division to multiplication
- Flip (reciprocal) the second fraction"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[3]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Dividing Mixed Numbers{note:"Convert to improper fractions, then divide.

Examples:
- 2⅓ ÷ 1½ = 7/3 ÷ 3/2 = 7/3 × 2/3 = 14/9 = 1⅚
- 3¾ ÷ 1¼ = 15/4 ÷ 5/4 = 15/4 × 4/5 = 60/20 = 3
- 2½ ÷ 1¼ = 5/2 ÷ 5/4 = 5/2 × 4/5 = 20/10 = 2
- 4⅔ ÷ 1⅓ = 14/3 ÷ 4/3 = 14/3 × 3/4 = 42/12 = 7/2 = 3½
- 3½ ÷ 2¼ = 7/2 ÷ 9/4 = 7/2 × 4/9 = 28/18 = 14/9 = 1⅚
- 5¼ ÷ 1½ = 21/4 ÷ 3/2 = 21/4 × 2/3 = 42/12 = 7/2 = 3½

Step-by-step:
1. Convert mixed numbers to improper fractions
2. Multiply by the reciprocal of the divisor
3. Simplify the result
4. Convert back to mixed number if needed"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]Ratios, Proportions, and Percentages{note:"Ratios compare quantities, proportions express equality of ratios, and percentages represent parts per hundred. These concepts are fundamental to understanding proportional relationships in all areas of life."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]Ratios{note:"A ratio compares two quantities by division. It can be written as a:b, a/b, or 'a to b'. Ratios can be simplified like fractions by dividing both terms by the greatest common factor. Ratios are used to compare populations, ingredient ratios in recipes, mixture concentrations, and many other proportional relationships."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing ratios and their simplification."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Understanding Ratios{note:"A ratio compares two quantities using division.

Notation:
- 3:2 (three to two)
- 3/2 (as a fraction)
- 3 to 2 (in words)

Basic examples:
- 3:2 means 3 parts to 2 parts
- If a recipe has ratio 3:2, and you have 6 of first, you need 4 of second
- 6:4 simplifies to 3:2 (divide by 2)
- 10:15 simplifies to 2:3 (divide by 5)
- 12:18 simplifies to 2:3 (divide by 6)
- 24:36 simplifies to 2:3 (divide by 12)

Ratio vs fraction:
- Ratio 3:2 compares two quantities
- Fraction 3/2 is one quantity divided by another
- 3:2 = 3/2 (as a division)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Real-World Ratio Applications{note:"Ratios appear in many real-world situations.

Classroom:
- A class has 18 boys and 12 girls
- Ratio boys:girls = 18:12 = 3:2
- Ratio girls:boys = 12:18 = 2:3
- Ratio boys:total = 18:30 = 3:5

Fuel efficiency:
- A car travels 240 miles on 8 gallons
- Ratio 240:8 = 30:1 (30 miles per gallon)
- This is a rate (miles per gallon)

Recipes:
- 2 cups flour : 1 cup sugar : 3 eggs
- This is a ratio of ingredients
- Scaling up: 4:2:6 for twice the recipe

Paint mixing:
- 3 parts white : 2 parts blue
- Total 5 parts
- White = 3/5 of mixture, Blue = 2/5 of mixture

Scores:
- A team scores 5 goals and concedes 2
- Goal ratio: 5:2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Solving Ratio Problems{note:"Use ratios to find unknown quantities.

Example 1:
- If ratio is 3:5 and first part is 12, find second part
- 3:5 = 12:x
- 3/5 = 12/x
- 3x = 60
- x = 20

Example 2:
- If ratio is 2:7 and total is 45, find the parts
- 2x + 7x = 45
- 9x = 45
- x = 5
- Parts are 10 and 35

Example 3:
- If ratio is 5:8 and total is 91
- 5x + 8x = 91
- 13x = 91
- x = 7
- Parts are 35 and 56

Example 4:
- If a recipe requires 2 cups flour : 3 cups sugar, how much sugar for 5 cups flour?
- 2:3 = 5:x
- 2/3 = 5/x
- 2x = 15
- x = 7.5 cups sugar"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Extended Ratios{note:"Ratios with more than two parts.

Example 1:
- 3:4:5 (three parts)
- If total is 60:
  3x + 4x + 5x = 60
  12x = 60
  x = 5
  Parts: 15, 20, 25

Example 2:
- Angles of a triangle in ratio 1:2:3
- Sum of angles = 180°
- x + 2x + 3x = 180
- 6x = 180
- x = 30
- Angles: 30°, 60°, 90°

Example 3:
- A school has students in ratio 2:3:5 for grades 6,7,8
- Total 120 students
- 2x + 3x + 5x = 120
- 10x = 120
- x = 12
- Grades: 24, 36, 60

Example 4:
- A company divides profits in ratio 4:3:2
- Total profit = $90,000
- 4x + 3x + 2x = 90000
- 9x = 90000
- x = 10000
- Shares: $40,000, $30,000, $20,000"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Simplifying Ratios with Units{note:"Ratios with different units must be converted to the same unit.

Example 1:
- 2 hours : 45 minutes
- Convert to minutes: 120 minutes : 45 minutes
- 120:45 = 8:3 (divide by 15)

Example 2:
- 1 meter : 25 cm
- Convert to cm: 100 cm : 25 cm
- 100:25 = 4:1 (divide by 25)

Example 3:
- $2 : 50 cents
- Convert to cents: 200 cents : 50 cents
- 200:50 = 4:1 (divide by 50)

Example 4:
- 3 kilometers : 500 meters
- Convert to meters: 3000m : 500m
- 3000:500 = 6:1 (divide by 500)

Rule: Always use the same units before simplifying."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]Proportions{note:"A proportion states that two ratios are equal: a:b = c:d or a/b = c/d. Cross multiplication: a×d = b×c. Proportions are used to solve for unknown quantities in proportional relationships, scale drawings, similar figures, and many real-world problems. They are essential for understanding direct and inverse relationships."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing proportions and solving for unknowns."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Solving Proportions{note:"Use cross multiplication to solve.

Examples:
- 3/4 = x/8 → 4x = 24 → x = 6
- 2/5 = 8/x → 2x = 40 → x = 20
- x/7 = 12/21 → 21x = 84 → x = 4
- 6/9 = x/15 → 9x = 90 → x = 10
- 5/8 = 15/x → 5x = 120 → x = 24
- 3/7 = x/28 → 7x = 84 → x = 12
- 4/9 = 12/x → 4x = 108 → x = 27

Cross multiplication:
- For a/b = c/d, multiply a×d = b×c

Example: 3/5 = 9/15
- 3×15 = 45, 5×9 = 45
- True proportion

Example: 2/3 = 5/8
- 2×8 = 16, 3×5 = 15
- Not a proportion"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Scale Drawings{note:"Proportions are used in scale drawings and maps.

Map scale:
- Map scale: 1 inch = 5 miles
- If distance on map is 3 inches, actual distance = 3×5 = 15 miles
- Proportion: 1/5 = 3/x → x = 15

Scale drawing:
- Scale: 1 cm = 10 km
- 4.5 cm on map → 45 km actual
- 1/10 = 4.5/x → x = 45

Architecture:
- Blueprint scale: 1/4 inch = 1 foot
- If a wall measures 3 inches on blueprint:
  0.25/1 = 3/x → 0.25x = 3 → x = 12 feet

Model cars:
- Model scale: 1:18
- If actual car is 4.5 m long, model length = 4.5/18 = 0.25 m = 25 cm"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Recipe Scaling{note:"Proportions scale recipes up or down.

Example 1:
- Recipe for 4 people uses 2 cups flour
- For 6 people: 2/4 = x/6 → 4x = 12 → x = 3 cups
- For 8 people: 2/4 = x/8 → 4x = 16 → x = 4 cups

Example 2:
- Recipe for 6 people uses 3 eggs and 4 cups flour
- For 9 people: 3/6 = x/9 → 6x = 27 → x = 4.5 eggs
  4/6 = y/9 → 6y = 36 → y = 6 cups flour

Example 3:
- A recipe calls for 2 cups sugar for 5 cups flour
- For 15 cups flour: 2/5 = x/15 → 5x = 30 → x = 6 cups sugar

Example 4:
- If a recipe makes 12 cookies and uses 2 cups flour
- How much flour for 30 cookies?
  2/12 = x/30 → 12x = 60 → x = 5 cups"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Similar Figures{note:"Proportions are used with similar figures.

Similar triangles:
- Corresponding sides are proportional
- If triangle has sides 3,4,5 and similar triangle has side 6 corresponding to 3:
  3/6 = 4/x = 5/y
- x = 8, y = 10

Scale factor:
- If one triangle has sides 3,4,5 and another has sides 6,8,10
- Scale factor = 2 (all sides doubled)
- Ratio of areas = scale factor² = 4
- Ratio of volumes = scale factor³ = 8

Real-world:
- A 6-foot tall person casts a 4-foot shadow
- A building casts a 30-foot shadow
- 6/4 = h/30 → 4h = 180 → h = 45 feet

Photography:
- Enlarging a photo: if original is 4×6 and enlarged to 8×12, scale factor = 2"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Direct and Inverse Proportion in Proportions{note:"Proportions can represent direct or inverse relationships.

Direct proportion:
- y = kx, y/x = k (constant)
- If x doubles, y doubles
- Example: cost of items: 2 apples cost $1, 4 apples cost $2
- 1/2 = 2/4 (proportion)

Direct proportion problems:
- A car travels at 60 mph. Distance = 60t
- If distance is 120 miles, time = 2 hours
- 60/1 = 120/2 (proportion)

Inverse proportion:
- y = k/x, xy = k (constant)
- If x doubles, y halves
- Example: speed and time: 120 miles at 30 mph = 4 hours, at 60 mph = 2 hours
- 30×4 = 60×2 = 120 (constant)

Inverse proportion problems:
- 6 workers build a wall in 8 hours
- 12 workers take 4 hours
- 6×8 = 12×4 = 48 (constant)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]Direct Proportion{note:"Two quantities are in direct proportion when they increase or decrease together at the same rate: y = kx, where k is the constant of proportionality. If x doubles, y doubles. Examples: cost of items at a constant price, distance at constant speed, weight and mass at a constant density. Direct proportion is fundamental in many mathematical and scientific relationships."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing direct proportion relationships and calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Cost and Quantity{note:"Direct proportion in pricing.

Example 1:
- 2 apples cost $1
- 4 apples cost $2
- 6 apples cost $3
- y = 0.5x (x = apples, y = dollars)
- k = 0.5 (price per apple)

- If 10 apples: y = 0.5×10 = $5
- If $3.50: x = 3.50/0.5 = 7 apples

Example 2:
- 5 pounds of rice cost $12.50
- 8 pounds cost: cost per pound = 12.50/5 = 2.50
- 8 pounds = 8×2.50 = $20
- k = 2.50 (dollars per pound)

Example 3:
- 3 pencils cost $0.75
- 7 pencils cost: 0.75/3 = 0.25 per pencil
- 7 pencils = 7×0.25 = $1.75"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Speed and Distance{note:"Direct proportion in motion.

Example 1:
- Car travels at 60 mph
- In 2 hours: 120 miles
- In 3 hours: 180 miles
- In 4.5 hours: 270 miles
- d = 60t (d = distance, t = time)

- If distance is 300 miles: t = 300/60 = 5 hours
- If time is 3.5 hours: d = 60×3.5 = 210 miles

Example 2:
- Bicycle speed = 15 mph
- Distance in 2.5 hours = 15×2.5 = 37.5 miles
- Time for 45 miles = 45/15 = 3 hours

Example 3:
- Running speed = 8 km/h
- Distance in 45 minutes (0.75 h) = 8×0.75 = 6 km"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Pay and Hours Worked{note:"Direct proportion in wages.

Example 1:
- Working $15/hour
- 8 hours = $120
- 10 hours = $150
- y = 15x (x = hours, y = dollars)

- If paid $225: hours = 225/15 = 15 hours
- If worked 6.5 hours: pay = 15×6.5 = $97.50

Example 2:
- $18/hour for 5 hours = $90
- For 7 hours: 18×7 = $126
- For $150: 150/18 = 8.33 hours

Example 3:
- A contractor charges $200 for 4 hours
- Rate = 200/4 = $50/hour
- 6 hours: 50×6 = $300"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Unit Conversion{note:"Direct proportion in unit conversion.

Example 1:
- 1 inch = 2.54 cm
- 10 inches = 10×2.54 = 25.4 cm
- 50 cm = 50/2.54 = 19.69 inches

Example 2:
- 1 foot = 12 inches
- 5 feet = 60 inches
- 3.5 feet = 3.5×12 = 42 inches
- 72 inches = 72/12 = 6 feet

Example 3:
- 1 kg = 2.20462 lb
- 10 kg = 10×2.20462 = 22.0462 lb
- 5 lb = 5/2.20462 = 2.268 kg

Example 4:
- 1 L = 1000 mL
- 2.5 L = 2500 mL
- 750 mL = 0.75 L"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Finding the Constant of Proportionality{note:"The constant k = y/x when y ∝ x.

Example 1:
- If y is directly proportional to x and y=6 when x=2:
  k = y/x = 6/2 = 3
  Equation: y = 3x

- When x=5: y = 3×5 = 15
- When y=21: 21 = 3x → x = 7

Example 2:
- If y ∝ x and y=12 when x=4:
  k = 12/4 = 3
  y = 3x

- When x=7: y = 21
- When y=18: 18 = 3x → x = 6

Example 3:
- If weight is directly proportional to mass: W = mg
  For mass 2 kg, weight 19.6 N
  k = 19.6/2 = 9.8
  W = 9.8m

- For mass 5 kg: W = 9.8×5 = 49 N"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]Inverse Proportion{note:"Two quantities are in inverse proportion when one increases as the other decreases: y = k/x, where k is the constant of proportionality. If x doubles, y halves. Examples: speed and time for a fixed distance, number of workers and time to complete a job, pressure and volume of a gas. Inverse proportion is crucial in physics and many optimization problems."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing inverse proportion relationships and calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Speed and Time{note:"Inverse proportion with fixed distance.

Example 1:
- Distance fixed at 120 miles
- At 30 mph: time = 4 hours
- At 40 mph: time = 3 hours
- At 60 mph: time = 2 hours
- At 120 mph: time = 1 hour
- t = 120/s (s = speed, t = time)

- If speed = 50 mph: t = 120/50 = 2.4 hours
- If time = 2.4 hours: s = 120/2.4 = 50 mph

Example 2:
- Distance fixed at 240 miles
- Speed 60 mph: time 4 hours
- Speed 80 mph: time 3 hours
- Speed 120 mph: time 2 hours

Example 3:
- 5 km run at 10 km/h: time = 0.5 hours
- At 8 km/h: time = 5/8 = 0.625 hours"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Workers and Time{note:"Inverse proportion in completing a job.

Example 1:
- 6 workers build a wall in 8 hours
- 12 workers (double) take 4 hours (half)
- 8 workers take 6 hours
- t = k/w (k = 6×8 = 48)
- For 4 workers: t = 48/4 = 12 hours
- For 16 workers: t = 48/16 = 3 hours

Example 2:
- 4 painters paint a house in 6 hours
- Time = 24/h (k = 4×6 = 24)
- 3 painters: 24/3 = 8 hours
- 8 painters: 24/8 = 3 hours

Example 3:
- 5 machines produce 1000 units in 8 hours
- 8 machines produce 1000 units in 5 hours
- k = 5×8 = 40 machine-hours"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Boyle's Law{note:"Inverse proportion in physics.

Boyle's Law:
- Pressure × Volume = constant (for gas at constant temperature)
- P₁V₁ = P₂V₂

Example 1:
- If P=2 atm, V=5 L: PV=10
- If P=4 atm: V = 10/4 = 2.5 L
- If V=10 L: P = 10/10 = 1 atm

Example 2:
- A gas at 3 atm occupies 4 L
- What volume at 6 atm?
  P₁V₁ = P₂V₂
  3×4 = 6×V₂
  V₂ = 12/6 = 2 L

Example 3:
- A gas at 2.5 atm occupies 8 L
- What pressure for 5 L?
  2.5×8 = P₂×5
  P₂ = 20/5 = 4 atm"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Inverse Proportion Problems{note:"Applications of inverse proportion.

Example 1:
- A number of people share a sum of money
- 10 people get $30 each: total $300
- 15 people get $20 each: 300/15 = 20
- 20 people get $15 each: 300/20 = 15
- k = 10×30 = 300

Example 2:
- A car journey takes 4 hours at 50 mph
- Time at 60 mph: 4×50/60 = 200/60 = 3.33 hours
- Time at 40 mph: 4×50/40 = 200/40 = 5 hours

Example 3:
- 5 students complete a project in 12 days
- 6 students: 5×12/6 = 10 days
- 10 students: 60/10 = 6 days

Example 4:
- A factory produces 1000 items in 8 hours with 5 machines
- With 10 machines: 5×8/10 = 4 hours"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Finding the Constant of Inverse Proportion{note:"The constant k = xy when y ∝ 1/x.

Example 1:
- If y is inversely proportional to x and y=6 when x=4:
  k = xy = 6×4 = 24
  Equation: y = 24/x

- When x=3: y = 24/3 = 8
- When x=8: y = 24/8 = 3
- When y=12: 12 = 24/x → x = 2

Example 2:
- If y ∝ 1/x and y=10 when x=5:
  k = 10×5 = 50
  y = 50/x

- When x=10: y = 50/10 = 5
- When y=25: 25 = 50/x → x = 2

Example 3:
- If speed and time are inversely proportional:
  speed = k/time
  At 60 mph, time = 2 hours: k = 60×2 = 120
  At 40 mph: t = 120/40 = 3 hours"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]Percentages{note:"A percentage represents a fraction with denominator 100: x% = x/100. Percentages are used everywhere: discounts, taxes, grades, statistics, and financial calculations. They provide a standardized way to express proportions and compare different quantities. Understanding percentages is essential for everyday financial literacy."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Comprehensive examples showing percentages and their calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Percentage Conversions{note:"Convert between percentages, decimals, and fractions.

Percent to decimal:
- 50% = 0.5
- 25% = 0.25
- 75% = 0.75
- 10% = 0.1
- 20% = 0.2
- 100% = 1
- 1% = 0.01
- 0.5% = 0.005

Percent to fraction:
- 50% = 1/2
- 25% = 1/4
- 75% = 3/4
- 10% = 1/10
- 20% = 1/5
- 33⅓% = 1/3
- 66⅔% = 2/3
- 100% = 1

Fraction to percent:
- 1/2 = 50%
- 1/4 = 25%
- 3/4 = 75%
- 1/3 = 33⅓%
- 2/3 = 66⅔%
- 1/5 = 20%
- 1/8 = 12.5%
- 1/10 = 10%"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Finding a Percentage of a Number{note:"Multiply the number by the decimal form of the percentage.

Examples:
- 25% of 80 = 0.25×80 = 20
- 15% of 200 = 0.15×200 = 30
- 120% of 50 = 1.2×50 = 60
- 0.5% of 400 = 0.005×400 = 2
- 8% of 150 = 0.08×150 = 12
- 30% of 250 = 0.3×250 = 75
- 7.5% of 200 = 0.075×200 = 15
- 10% of 350 = 35

Mental math:
- 10% of 80 = 8 (move decimal one place)
- 20% of 80 = 16 (double 10%)
- 5% of 80 = 4 (half of 10%)
- 15% of 80 = 12 (10% + 5%)
- 25% of 80 = 20 (quarter)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Finding What Percentage One Number is of Another{note:"Divide the part by the whole and multiply by 100%.

Examples:
- What % of 80 is 20? 20/80 × 100% = 25%
- What % of 150 is 30? 30/150 × 100% = 20%
- 45 is what % of 60? 45/60 × 100% = 75%
- 12 is what % of 48? 12/48 × 100% = 25%
- 15 is what % of 75? 15/75 × 100% = 20%
- 8 is what % of 40? 8/40 × 100% = 20%
- 25 is what % of 125? 25/125 × 100% = 20%
- 50 is what % of 200? 50/200 × 100% = 25%

Real-world:
- If you scored 45 out of 50: 45/50 × 100% = 90%
- If you ate 12 out of 16 cookies: 12/16 × 100% = 75%
- If 15 students out of 20 are present: 15/20 × 100% = 75%"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Finding the Whole from a Percentage{note:"Divide the part by the decimal form of the percentage.

Examples:
- 25 is 20% of what number? 25 = 0.2x → x = 125
- 30 is 15% of what number? 30 = 0.15x → x = 200
- 45 is 30% of what number? 45 = 0.3x → x = 150
- 12 is 40% of what number? 12 = 0.4x → x = 30
- 18 is 75% of what number? 18 = 0.75x → x = 24
- 21 is 35% of what number? 21 = 0.35x → x = 60

Real-world:
- If $15 is 20% of the total bill, total = 15/0.2 = $75
- If 25 students are 50% of the class, class size = 25/0.5 = 50
- If a 15% discount saved you $30, original price = 30/0.15 = $200"}
