lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]Percentage Increase{note:"Percentage increase measures the relative growth of a quantity: (Increase/Original) × 100%. It is widely used for price increases, population growth, salary raises, and many other growth scenarios. Understanding percentage increase helps in comparing growth rates across different contexts."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing percentage increase calculations and applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Percentage Increase{note:"Calculate increase and percentage.

Formula:
- Percentage increase = (increase / original) × 100%

Examples:
- From $40 to $48: increase = $8, 8/40×100 = 20%
- From $50 to $65: increase = $15, 15/50×100 = 30%
- From 80 to 100: increase = 20, 20/80×100 = 25%
- From 120 to 150: increase = 30, 30/120×100 = 25%
- From 200 to 250: increase = 50, 50/200×100 = 25%
- From 45 to 54: increase = 9, 9/45×100 = 20%

Step-by-step:
1. Find the increase: new - original
2. Divide by original
3. Multiply by 100% to get percentage"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Real-World Percentage Increase{note:"Applications of percentage increase.

Population growth:
- Town grew from 5,000 to 5,800
- Increase = 800, 800/5000×100 = 16% increase
- City from 100,000 to 115,000: 15,000/100,000×100 = 15%
- Village from 2,500 to 3,000: 500/2500×100 = 20%

Salary:
- $45,000 to $50,400: increase=5,400, 5400/45000×100 = 12%
- $30,000 to $33,000: 3,000/30,000×100 = 10%
- $60,000 to $66,000: 6,000/60,000×100 = 10%

Price:
- Gas from $3.50 to $3.85: 0.35/3.50×100 = 10%
- Rent from $800 to $880: 80/800×100 = 10%
- Stock from $50 to $75: 25/50×100 = 50%

Height:
- 150 cm to 165 cm: 15/150×100 = 10%
- 5 ft to 5.5 ft: 0.5/5×100 = 10%"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Finding Original Value After Increase{note:"Work backwards from the final value.

Formula:
- Original = Final / (1 + rate)

Examples:
- After 20% increase, price is $120
  Original = 120/1.2 = $100
- After 15% increase, salary is $57,500
  Original = 57,500/1.15 = $50,000
- After 25% increase, value is 150
  Original = 150/1.25 = 120
- After 10% increase, price is $220
  Original = 220/1.1 = $200
- After 5% increase, rent is $1,050
  Original = 1050/1.05 = $1000

Step-by-step:
1. The final value = original × (1 + rate)
2. Original = final / (1 + rate)
3. Rate expressed as a decimal"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Finding New Value After Increase{note:"Calculate the new value directly.

Formula:
- New = Original × (1 + rate)

Examples:
- $100 increased by 20%: 100 × 1.2 = $120
- $50 increased by 15%: 50 × 1.15 = $57.50
- 80 increased by 25%: 80 × 1.25 = 100
- 200 increased by 10%: 200 × 1.1 = 220
- 150 increased by 5%: 150 × 1.05 = 157.50

Method:
1. Convert percentage to decimal: 20% = 0.2
2. Add 1: 1 + 0.2 = 1.2
3. Multiply original by this factor"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Successive Percentage Increases{note:"Multiple increases applied sequentially.

Example 1:
- A price increases by 10% then another 10%
- $100 → $110 → $121
- Total increase = 21%, not 20% (compounding)

Example 2:
- A salary increases by 5% then another 5%
- $50,000 → $52,500 → $55,125
- Total increase = 10.25%, not 10%

Example 3:
- 20% increase followed by 10% increase
- $100 → $120 → $132
- Total increase = 32%

Example 4:
- Three 10% increases
- $100 → $110 → $121 → $133.10
- Total increase = 33.1%

Rule: Successive percentage increases compound, not add."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]Percentage Decrease{note:"Percentage decrease measures the relative reduction of a quantity: (Decrease/Original) × 100%. It is used for discounts, price drops, population decline, and depreciation. Like increase, it allows standardized comparison of reductions across different quantities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing percentage decrease calculations and applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Percentage Decrease{note:"Calculate decrease and percentage.

Formula:
- Percentage decrease = (decrease / original) × 100%

Examples:
- From $80 to $60: decrease = $20, 20/80×100 = 25%
- From $100 to $75: decrease = 25, 25/100×100 = 25%
- From $200 to $160: decrease = 40, 40/200×100 = 20%
- From 120 to 90: decrease = 30, 30/120×100 = 25%
- From 50 to 40: decrease = 10, 10/50×100 = 20%
- From 75 to 60: decrease = 15, 15/75×100 = 20%

Step-by-step:
1. Find the decrease: original - new
2. Divide by original
3. Multiply by 100% to get percentage"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Real-World Percentage Decrease{note:"Applications of percentage decrease.

Discounts:
- $80 item on sale for $60: 20/80×100 = 25% off
- $100 item on sale for $75: 25/100×100 = 25% off
- $200 item on sale for $160: 40/200×100 = 20% off
- $250 item on sale for $200: 50/250×100 = 20% off

Population decline:
- Town from 8,000 to 7,200: 800/8000×100 = 10%
- City from 200,000 to 180,000: 20,000/200,000×100 = 10%
- Village from 1,500 to 1,200: 300/1500×100 = 20%

Weight loss:
- 200 lbs to 170 lbs: 30/200×100 = 15%
- 150 lbs to 135 lbs: 15/150×100 = 10%
- 250 lbs to 200 lbs: 50/250×100 = 20%

Depreciation:
- Car from $20,000 to $17,000: 3,000/20,000×100 = 15%
- Equipment from $5,000 to $4,000: 1,000/5,000×100 = 20%"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Finding Original Value After Decrease{note:"Work backwards from the final value.

Formula:
- Original = Final / (1 - rate)

Examples:
- After 20% discount, price is $80
  Original = 80/0.8 = $100
- After 15% decrease, value is 85
  Original = 85/0.85 = 100
- After 30% decrease, value is 210
  Original = 210/0.7 = 300
- After 10% discount, price is $45
  Original = 45/0.9 = $50
- After 25% decrease, value is 150
  Original = 150/0.75 = 200

Step-by-step:
1. The final value = original × (1 - rate)
2. Original = final / (1 - rate)
3. Rate expressed as a decimal"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Finding New Value After Decrease{note:"Calculate the new value directly.

Formula:
- New = Original × (1 - rate)

Examples:
- $100 decreased by 20%: 100 × 0.8 = $80
- $50 decreased by 15%: 50 × 0.85 = $42.50
- 80 decreased by 25%: 80 × 0.75 = 60
- 200 decreased by 10%: 200 × 0.9 = 180
- 150 decreased by 5%: 150 × 0.95 = 142.50

Sale price calculations:
- Original $120, 25% off: 0.75×120 = $90
- Original $75, 20% off: 0.8×75 = $60
- Original $250, 15% off: 0.85×250 = $212.50
- Original $100, 10% off: 0.9×100 = $90

Method:
1. Convert percentage to decimal: 20% = 0.2
2. Subtract from 1: 1 - 0.2 = 0.8
3. Multiply original by this factor"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Successive Percentage Decreases{note:"Multiple decreases applied sequentially.

Example 1:
- 20% off followed by another 10% off
- $100 → $80 → $72
- Total discount = 28%, not 30%

Example 2:
- 10% off followed by another 10% off
- $100 → $90 → $81
- Total discount = 19%, not 20%

Example 3:
- 50% off followed by another 20% off
- $100 → $50 → $40
- Total discount = 60%, not 70%

Example 4:
- 25% off then 10% off
- $100 → $75 → $67.50
- Total discount = 32.5%

Rule: Successive percentage decreases compound, not add.
- A decrease followed by another decrease is not the sum of the percentages.
- The second discount is applied to the reduced price."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]Compound Percentage{note:"Compound percentage involves applying a percentage increase or decrease repeatedly, where each new percentage is applied to the new value (not the original). This is the foundation of compound interest, population growth, inflation, and many real-world growth processes. The formula is Final = Initial × (1 ± r)^n where r is the rate and n is the number of periods."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing compound percentage calculations and applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Compound Interest{note:"Compound interest grows money exponentially.

Formula:
- A = P(1 + r)^n (annual compounding)
- P = principal, r = rate, n = number of years

Example 1:
- $1000 at 5% per year for 3 years
  Year 1: 1000×1.05=1050
  Year 2: 1050×1.05=1102.50
  Year 3: 1102.50×1.05=1157.625
  Formula: 1000×(1.05)³ = 1157.63

Example 2:
- $2000 at 4% for 5 years
  A = 2000×(1.04)⁵ = 2433.31

Example 3:
- $5000 at 6% for 10 years
  A = 5000×(1.06)¹⁰ = 8954.24

Compare with simple interest:
- Simple: 1000 + 1000×0.05×3 = 1150
- Compound: 1157.63 (difference of $7.63 after 3 years)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Different Compounding Periods{note:"More frequent compounding yields slightly more.

Formula:
- A = P(1 + r/n)^(nt)
- n = number of compounding periods per year

Example: $1000 at 6% for 2 years
- Annual (n=1): 1000×(1.06)² = 1123.60
- Semi-annual (n=2): 1000×(1+0.06/2)^(2×2) = 1000×(1.03)⁴ = 1125.51
- Quarterly (n=4): 1000×(1+0.06/4)^8 = 1000×(1.015)⁸ = 1126.49
- Monthly (n=12): 1000×(1+0.06/12)^24 = 1000×(1.005)²⁴ = 1127.16
- Daily (n=365): 1000×(1+0.06/365)^730 ≈ 1127.50

Continuous compounding:
- A = P×e^(rt) = 1000×e^(0.12) ≈ 1127.50

More frequent compounding approaches the continuous limit."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Population Growth{note:"Compound growth in populations.

Example 1:
- 5000 population grows 10% per year for 3 years
  Year 1: 5000×1.1=5500
  Year 2: 5500×1.1=6050
  Year 3: 6050×1.1=6655
  Formula: 5000×(1.1)³ = 6655

- After 5 years: 5000×1.1⁵ = 8052.55
- After 10 years: 5000×1.1¹⁰ = 12,968.71

Example 2:
- 1000 bacteria double every hour (100% growth)
  After 1 hour: 2000
  After 2 hours: 4000
  After 3 hours: 8000
  Formula: 1000×2^n

Example 3:
- 10,000 city population grows 2% per year
  After 5 years: 10000×(1.02)⁵ = 11,040.81
  After 10 years: 10000×(1.02)¹⁰ = 12,189.94"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Depreciation{note:"Compound decrease in value.

Formula:
- Value = Initial × (1 - r)^n

Example 1:
- $20,000 car depreciates 15% per year for 3 years
  Year 1: 20000×0.85=17000
  Year 2: 17000×0.85=14450
  Year 3: 14450×0.85=12282.50
  Formula: 20000×0.85³ = 12282.50

- After 5 years: 20000×0.85⁵ = 8874.11

Example 2:
- $50,000 equipment depreciates 10% per year
  After 4 years: 50000×0.9⁴ = 50000×0.6561 = 32,805

Example 3:
- $500,000 house depreciates 3% per year (in some markets)
  After 5 years: 500000×0.97⁵ = 500000×0.8587 = 429,350"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Finding Rate or Time{note:"Solve for the rate or time in compound growth.

Finding rate:
- $1000 grows to $1210 in 2 years
  1210 = 1000×(1+r)²
  (1+r)² = 1.21
  1+r = 1.1
  r = 0.1 = 10%

Finding time:
- $1000 grows to $1500 at 5%
  1500 = 1000×(1.05)^t
  1.5 = (1.05)^t
  t = ln(1.5)/ln(1.05) ≈ 8.31 years

Rule of 72:
- Approximate doubling time: t ≈ 72/r (where r is percentage rate)
- At 6%: doubling time ≈ 72/6 = 12 years
  Actual: 1.06¹² = 2.012 ≈ 2
- At 9%: doubling time ≈ 8 years
  Actual: 1.09⁸ ≈ 1.993
- At 12%: doubling time ≈ 6 years
  Actual: 1.12⁶ ≈ 1.974

The Rule of 72 is a useful approximation for quick mental calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]Simple Interest{note:"Simple interest is calculated only on the principal amount: I = P × r × t, where P is principal, r is rate (decimal), and t is time in years. Total amount = P + I = P(1 + rt). Simple interest is used for short-term loans, bonds, and basic financial calculations. Unlike compound interest, it doesn't earn interest on interest."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing simple interest calculations and applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Simple Interest{note:"Calculate interest and total amount.

Formula:
- I = P × r × t
- Total = P + I = P(1 + rt)

Examples:
- $1000 at 6% for 3 years
  I = 1000×0.06×3 = 180
  Total = 1000+180=1180

- $500 at 8% for 5 years
  I = 500×0.08×5 = 200
  Total = 700

- $2000 at 4.5% for 2 years
  I = 2000×0.045×2 = 180
  Total = 2180

- $3000 at 5% for 4 years
  I = 3000×0.05×4 = 600
  Total = 3600

- $1500 at 7% for 3 years
  I = 1500×0.07×3 = 315
  Total = 1815"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Finding Rate or Time{note:"Solve for missing variables in simple interest.

Finding rate:
- I=120, P=800, t=2 years
  r = I/(P×t) = 120/(800×2) = 0.075 = 7.5%

- I=180, P=1000, t=3 years
  r = 180/(1000×3) = 0.06 = 6%

Finding time:
- I=200, P=1000, r=5% = 0.05
  t = I/(P×r) = 200/(1000×0.05) = 4 years

- I=360, P=2000, r=6% = 0.06
  t = 360/(2000×0.06) = 360/120 = 3 years

Finding principal:
- I=360, r=6% = 0.06, t=3 years
  P = I/(r×t) = 360/(0.06×3) = 360/0.18 = 2000

- I=150, r=5% = 0.05, t=2 years
  P = 150/(0.05×2) = 150/0.1 = 1500"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Real-World Simple Interest{note:"Applications of simple interest.

Loans:
- Loan $5000 at 7% for 2 years
  I = 5000×0.07×2 = 700
  Total to pay back = $5700

- Loan $10,000 at 8% for 3 years
  I = 10000×0.08×3 = 2400
  Total = $12,400

- Short-term loan $1000 at 10% for 6 months (0.5 years)
  I = 1000×0.10×0.5 = $50
  Total = $1050

Savings:
- Deposit $2000 at 3.5% for 4 years
  I = 2000×0.035×4 = 280
  Balance = $2280

- Deposit $5000 at 2.5% for 2 years
  I = 5000×0.025×2 = 250
  Balance = $5250

Bonds:
- $10,000 bond at 6% for 5 years
  I = 10000×0.06×5 = 3000
  Total = $13,000"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Simple vs Compound Interest{note:"Compare simple and compound interest.

Example: $1000 at 5% for 5 years
Simple interest:
- I = 1000×0.05×5 = 250
- Total = 1250

Compound interest (annual):
- Year 1: 1000×1.05=1050
- Year 2: 1050×1.05=1102.50
- Year 3: 1102.50×1.05=1157.63
- Year 4: 1157.63×1.05=1215.51
- Year 5: 1215.51×1.05=1276.28
- Total = 1276.28

Difference:
- Compound total: $1276.28
- Simple total: $1250.00
- Difference: $26.28

The difference grows with time and rate:
- At 10% for 10 years:
  Simple: 1000×0.10×10 = 2000 → total 3000
  Compound: 1000×1.1¹⁰ = 2593.74
  Difference = 593.74

Compound interest grows faster because it earns interest on interest."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Applications of Simple Interest{note:"Simple interest in everyday situations.

Student loans:
- $20,000 loan at 4% for 10 years (simple interest)
  I = 20000×0.04×10 = 8000
  Total = $28,000

Car loan:
- $15,000 car loan at 6% for 5 years
  I = 15000×0.06×5 = 4500
  Total = $19,500

Credit card:
- $1000 debt at 18% annual rate for 1 year
  I = 1000×0.18×1 = 180
  Total = $1180

Savings bonds:
- $1000 bond at 5% for 7 years (simple interest)
  I = 1000×0.05×7 = 350
  Total = $1350

Note: Many real-world loans use compound interest, but some use simple interest for short-term loans."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]Compound Interest{note:"Compound interest is calculated on the principal and accumulated interest: A = P(1 + r/n)^(nt), where r is rate, n is number of compounding periods per year, t is years. For annual compounding: A = P(1+r)^t. Compound interest is used for savings accounts, investments, loans, and many financial products. It's called the 'eighth wonder of the world' by financial experts."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing compound interest calculations with different compounding frequencies."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Annual Compounding{note:"Interest compounded once per year.

Formula:
- A = P(1+r)^t

Examples:
- $1000 at 5% for 3 years
  A = 1000×(1.05)³ = 1157.63

- $2000 at 4% for 5 years
  A = 2000×(1.04)⁵ = 2433.31

- $5000 at 6% for 10 years
  A = 5000×(1.06)¹⁰ = 8954.24

- $3000 at 8% for 4 years
  A = 3000×(1.08)⁴ = 3000×1.3605 = 4081.50

- $10,000 at 7% for 20 years
  A = 10000×(1.07)²⁰ = 10000×3.8697 = 38,697

Long-term growth:
- $1000 at 10% for 10 years: 1000×1.1¹⁰ = 2593.74
- $1000 at 10% for 20 years: 1000×1.1²⁰ = 6727.50
- $1000 at 10% for 30 years: 1000×1.1³⁰ = 17,449.40"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Different Compounding Frequencies{note:"More frequent compounding yields higher returns.

Formula:
- A = P(1 + r/n)^(nt)

Example: $1000 at 6% for 2 years
- Annual (n=1): 1000×(1.06)² = 1123.60
- Semi-annual (n=2): 1000×(1.03)⁴ = 1125.51
- Quarterly (n=4): 1000×(1.015)⁸ = 1126.49
- Monthly (n=12): 1000×(1.005)²⁴ = 1127.16
- Weekly (n=52): 1000×(1+0.06/52)^104 ≈ 1127.42
- Daily (n=365): 1000×(1+0.06/365)^730 ≈ 1127.49
- Continuous: 1000×e^(0.12) ≈ 1127.50

Example: $2000 at 5% for 5 years
- Annual: 2000×(1.05)⁵ = 2552.56
- Monthly: 2000×(1+0.05/12)^60 = 2000×(1.004167)^60 = 2566.77

The difference increases with larger principal, higher rate, and longer time."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Finding Future Value{note:"Calculate future value with compound interest.

Example 1:
- $10,000 at 8% for 20 years
  A = 10000×(1.08)²⁰ = 10000×4.66096 = 46,609.57

Example 2:
- $15,000 at 7% for 15 years
  A = 15000×(1.07)¹⁵ = 15000×2.75903 = 41,385.45

Example 3:
- $25,000 at 5% for 10 years
  A = 25000×(1.05)¹⁰ = 25000×1.62889 = 40,722.25

Example 4:
- $50,000 at 6% for 30 years
  A = 50000×(1.06)³⁰ = 50000×5.74349 = 287,174.50

Rule of 72 for doubling:
- At 6%: doubles in about 12 years
- At 8%: doubles in about 9 years
- At 10%: doubles in about 7.2 years
- At 12%: doubles in about 6 years"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Finding Present Value{note:"Calculate how much to invest now.

Formula:
- P = A/(1+r)^t (for annual compounding)

Examples:
- To have $10,000 in 5 years at 6%
  P = 10000/(1.06)⁵ = 10000/1.33823 = 7472.58
  Invest $7,472.58 today

- To have $50,000 in 10 years at 5%
  P = 50000/(1.05)¹⁰ = 50000/1.62889 = 30,695.66

- To have $100,000 in 20 years at 8%
  P = 100000/(1.08)²⁰ = 100000/4.66096 = 21,454.90

- To have $25,000 in 8 years at 4%
  P = 25000/(1.04)⁸ = 25000/1.36857 = 18,267.83

Present value is the amount you need to invest today to reach a future goal."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[4]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Real-World Compound Interest{note:"Compound interest in savings and investments.

Savings account:
- Deposit $10,000 at 3% interest
  After 10 years: 10000×1.03¹⁰ = 13,439
  After 20 years: 10000×1.03²⁰ = 18,061
  After 30 years: 10000×1.03³⁰ = 24,273

Retirement savings:
- $100/month (annual deposit $1200) at 8% for 30 years
  Using annuity formula: FV = PMT × ((1+r)^n - 1)/r
  FV = 1200 × ((1.08)^30 - 1)/0.08
  FV = 1200 × (10.0627 - 1)/0.08
  FV = 1200 × 113.283 = 135,939

- $200/month at 8% for 40 years
  FV = 2400 × ((1.08)^40 - 1)/0.08
  FV = 2400 × (21.7245 - 1)/0.08
  FV = 2400 × 259.056 = 621,734

Power of starting early:
- Age 25: $100/month for 40 years at 8% → $621,734
- Age 35: $100/month for 30 years at 8% → $135,939
- Starting 10 years earlier more than quadruples the final amount"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]Basic Geometry{note:"Geometry is the branch of mathematics dealing with shapes, sizes, positions, and properties of space. It is essential for understanding the physical world, architecture, engineering, art, and many practical applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[1]Points, Lines, Rays, Segments{note:"A point is a location with no size. A line is an infinite set of points extending in both directions. A ray has a starting point and extends infinitely in one direction. A line segment has two endpoints. These are the most basic geometric objects, forming the foundation of all geometry."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing points, lines, rays, and segments with notation."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Definitions{note:"Define and identify basic geometric objects.

Point:
- A location with no size
- Named with a capital letter: Point A
- Example: A dot on paper represents a point

Line:
- Infinite set of points extending in both directions
- Named with two points: Line AB or line l
- Example: A straight road extending forever in both directions

Ray:
- Has a starting point and extends infinitely in one direction
- Named starting point first: Ray AB (starts at A, passes through B)
- Example: A laser beam from a pointer

Segment:
- Has two endpoints
- Named with endpoints: Segment AB
- Example: A pencil (finite length)

Notation:
- Line AB: AB with arrows (↔)
- Ray AB: AB with arrow (→)
- Segment AB: AB with no arrows (—)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Relationships Between Lines{note:"How lines interact with each other.

Intersecting lines:
- Two lines that cross at a point
- They share exactly one point
- Example: The intersection of two streets

Parallel lines:
- Two lines that never meet
- They have the same direction
- Symbol: l ∥ m
- Example: Railroad tracks

Perpendicular lines:
- Lines that intersect at right angles (90°)
- Symbol: l ⊥ m
- Example: The corner of a square

Skew lines:
- Lines in 3D that are not parallel and don't intersect
- Example: Two edges of a box that don't meet

Concurrent lines:
- Three or more lines that intersect at the same point"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Points on a Line{note:"Collinear, between, and midpoint.

Collinear points:
- Points that lie on the same line
- A, B, C on line l are collinear
- Non-collinear: Points that do not lie on the same line

Betweenness:
- Point B is between A and C if A, B, C are collinear and AB + BC = AC
- Example: A---B---C

Segment addition postulate:
- If B is between A and C, then AB + BC = AC
- Example: If AB = 5 and BC = 7, then AC = 12

Midpoint:
- The point that divides the segment into two equal parts
- M is the midpoint of AB if AM = MB
- Example: If A at 0 and B at 10, midpoint at 5

Midpoint formula:
- M = ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2)
- Example: A(2,4), B(8,6) → M = (5,5)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[2]Angles{note:"An angle is formed by two rays sharing a common endpoint. Angles are measured in degrees. Types: acute (<90°), right (=90°), obtuse (>90° and <180°), straight (=180°), reflex (>180° and <360°). Angles are fundamental in geometry, trigonometry, and construction."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing different types of angles and angle relationships."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Types of Angles{note:"Angles classified by their measure.

Acute angle:
- Less than 90°
- Examples: 30°, 45°, 60°, 89°
- A sharp angle

Right angle:
- Exactly 90°
- Quarter turn
- Symbol: a small square at the vertex
- Example: Corner of a rectangle

Obtuse angle:
- Greater than 90° and less than 180°
- Examples: 120°, 135°, 150°, 170°
- A wide angle

Straight angle:
- Exactly 180°
- Half turn
- A straight line

Reflex angle:
- Greater than 180° and less than 360°
- Examples: 200°, 270°, 300°, 350°
- The larger angle around a point

Full angle:
- Exactly 360°
- Complete turn"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Complementary and Supplementary Angles{note:"Angle pairs that sum to specific values.

Complementary angles:
- Two angles whose sum is 90°
- 30° and 60° are complementary
- 45° and 45° are complementary
- 20° and 70° are complementary
- 15° and 75° are complementary

Supplementary angles:
- Two angles whose sum is 180°
- 70° and 110° are supplementary
- 120° and 60° are supplementary
- 45° and 135° are supplementary
- 30° and 150° are supplementary

Vertical angles:
- Two non-adjacent angles formed by intersecting lines
- Vertical angles are equal
- If one angle is 50°, the vertical angle is also 50°

Adjacent angles:
- Angles that share a vertex and a side
- They are next to each other
- Example: Two angles in a corner"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Angles with Parallel Lines{note:"Angles formed by a transversal cutting parallel lines.

Corresponding angles:
- Same position with respect to parallel lines and transversal
- Equal in measure
- Example: If one is 70°, the corresponding is 70°

Alternate interior angles:
- Lie between parallel lines on opposite sides of transversal
- Equal in measure
- Example: If one is 70°, the alternate interior is 70°

Alternate exterior angles:
- Lie outside parallel lines on opposite sides of transversal
- Equal in measure

Same-side interior angles:
- Lie between parallel lines on the same side of transversal
- Supplementary (sum to 180°)

Same-side exterior angles:
- Lie outside parallel lines on the same side of transversal
- Supplementary (sum to 180°)

If lines are parallel, these angle relationships hold."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[3]Polygons{note:"A polygon is a closed figure made of line segments. Polygons are classified by the number of sides: triangle (3), quadrilateral (4), pentagon (5), hexagon (6), octagon (8), decagon (10). Regular polygons have all sides and all angles equal. Irregular polygons have varying sides and angles."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing different polygons and their properties."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Polygons by Number of Sides{note:"Classification of polygons.

Triangle:
- 3 sides
- Sum of angles = 180°
- Interior angle sum formula: (3-2)×180 = 180°

Quadrilateral:
- 4 sides
- Sum of angles = 360°
- Interior angle sum formula: (4-2)×180 = 360°
- Examples: square, rectangle, parallelogram, trapezoid

Pentagon:
- 5 sides
- Sum of angles = 540°
- Interior angle sum formula: (5-2)×180 = 540°

Hexagon:
- 6 sides
- Sum of angles = 720°
- Interior angle sum formula: (6-2)×180 = 720°

Heptagon:
- 7 sides
- Sum of angles = 900°
- Interior angle sum formula: (7-2)×180 = 900°

Octagon:
- 8 sides
- Sum of angles = 1080°
- Interior angle sum formula: (8-2)×180 = 1080°

Decagon:
- 10 sides
- Sum of angles = 1440°
- Interior angle sum formula: (10-2)×180 = 1440°

n-gon:
- n sides
- Sum of angles = (n-2)×180°"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Regular vs Irregular Polygons{note:"Regular polygons have equal sides and angles.

Regular:
- All sides equal length
- All angles equal
- Examples: equilateral triangle, square, regular pentagon

Irregular:
- Sides and angles vary
- Examples: scalene triangle, rectangle, trapezoid

Regular polygon interior angle:
- Each interior angle = (n-2)×180/n
- Equilateral triangle: (3-2)×180/3 = 60°
- Square: (4-2)×180/4 = 90°
- Regular pentagon: (5-2)×180/5 = 108°
- Regular hexagon: (6-2)×180/6 = 120°
- Regular octagon: (8-2)×180/8 = 135°

Regular polygon exterior angle:
- Each exterior angle = 360/n
- Equilateral triangle: 360/3 = 120°
- Square: 360/4 = 90°
- Regular pentagon: 360/5 = 72°
- Regular hexagon: 360/6 = 60°
- Regular octagon: 360/8 = 45°"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Diagonals of Polygons{note:"A diagonal connects non-adjacent vertices.

Number of diagonals:
- Formula: d = n(n-3)/2
- Triangle: 3(3-3)/2 = 0 diagonals
- Quadrilateral: 4(4-3)/2 = 2 diagonals
- Pentagon: 5(5-3)/2 = 5 diagonals
- Hexagon: 6(6-3)/2 = 9 diagonals
- Heptagon: 7(7-3)/2 = 14 diagonals
- Octagon: 8(8-3)/2 = 20 diagonals
- Decagon: 10(10-3)/2 = 35 diagonals

Examples:
- A square has 2 diagonals that bisect each other
- A regular pentagon has 5 diagonals forming a star (pentagram)
- A regular hexagon has 9 diagonals"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]Circles{note:"A circle is the set of all points equidistant from a center point. Key parts: radius (center to edge), diameter (through center, twice radius), circumference (perimeter), area, arc (part of circumference), sector (slice of circle), chord (line segment connecting two points), tangent (line touching at one point), secant (line intersecting at two points). Circles are essential in geometry, trigonometry, and real-world applications from wheels to orbits."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing circle parts and their relationships."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Parts of a Circle{note:"Basic circle components.

Center:
- The point equidistant from all points on the circle
- Labeled O

Radius (r):
- Distance from center to any point on the circle
- All radii are equal

Diameter (d):
- Distance across the circle through the center
- d = 2r

Chord:
- A line segment joining any two points on the circle
- Diameter is the longest chord

Arc:
- Any part of the circumference
- Minor arc: less than 180°
- Major arc: greater than 180°

Sector:
- Region bounded by two radii and the arc between them
- Like a slice of pie

Segment:
- Region bounded by a chord and the arc between its endpoints

Tangent:
- A line that touches the circle at exactly one point
- Perpendicular to the radius at the point of tangency

Secant:
- A line that intersects the circle at two points"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Circle Measurements{note:"Formulas for circle measurements.

Circumference (C):
- C = 2πr = πd
- π ≈ 3.14159

Area (A):
- A = πr²

Arc length:
- For angle θ in degrees:
- Arc length = (θ/360) × 2πr
- For angle θ in radians:
- Arc length = θr

Sector area:
- For angle θ in degrees:
- Sector area = (θ/360) × πr²
- For angle θ in radians:
- Sector area = (1/2)θr²

Examples:
- Circle with r=5 cm:
  d = 10 cm
  C = 2π×5 = 31.4 cm
  A = π×25 = 78.5 cm²

- Sector with θ=60°, r=4 cm:
  Arc = (60/360)×2π×4 = 4.19 cm
  Sector area = (60/360)×π×16 = 8.38 cm²"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Angles in Circles{note:"Central and inscribed angles.

Central angle:
- Vertex at the center of the circle
- Sides are radii
- Measure equals the intercepted arc
- Example: A 90° central angle intercepts a 90° arc

Inscribed angle:
- Vertex on the circle
- Sides are chords
- Measure is half the intercepted arc
- Example: An inscribed angle that intercepts a 90° arc is 45°

Inscribed angle theorem:
- Inscribed angle = ½ × intercepted arc
- Inscribed angles that intercept the same arc are equal
- An inscribed angle that intercepts a diameter is 90°
  (Thales' theorem)

Example:
- A central angle of 80° intercepts an 80° arc
- An inscribed angle intercepting the same arc is 40°"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Tangents and Secants{note:"Lines intersecting circles.

Tangent:
- Touches circle at exactly one point
- Perpendicular to radius at point of tangency
- Tangent segments from the same external point are equal

Secant:
- Intersects circle at two points

Tangent-secant theorem:
- The square of the tangent length equals the product of the secant's external and internal parts
- Tangent² = External × Secant (whole)

Example:
- From point P outside circle, tangent PT = 6 cm
- Secant PA with external part 3 cm and internal part 9 cm
- 6² = 3 × 12 = 36 ✓

Tangent-radius relationship:
- Radius is perpendicular to tangent at the point of tangency
- This forms a right angle"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Applications of Circles{note:"Circles in real life.

Wheels:
- Radius determines distance per rotation
- Circumference = distance per revolution
- Example: A wheel with r=0.5 m travels 3.14 m per revolution

Orbits:
- Planets move in nearly circular orbits
- Satellites orbit Earth

Gears:
- Circular gears transmit rotational motion
- Gear ratio depends on circumferences

Pipes:
- Circular cross-section for efficient flow
- Area determines capacity

Everyday objects:
- Coins, wheels, plates, clocks
- Round tables, circular gardens
- Ferris wheels, carousels

Geometric constructions:
- Compass for drawing circles
- Angle bisectors and perpendiculars using circles"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]Three-Dimensional Shapes{note:"3D shapes extend geometry into three dimensions. Key shapes: cube (6 square faces), rectangular prism (6 rectangular faces), sphere (all points equidistant from center), cylinder (two circular bases), cone (circular base tapering to point), pyramid (polygon base with triangular faces), Platonic solids (regular polyhedra). Understanding 3D shapes is essential for architecture, packaging, and spatial reasoning."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing 3D shapes and their properties."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Cubes and Rectangular Prisms{note:"Rectangular solids with square or rectangular faces.

Cube:
- 6 square faces
- 8 vertices
- 12 edges
- All edges equal length (s)
- Volume: V = s³
- Surface area: SA = 6s²
- Example: s=3 → V=27, SA=54

Rectangular prism:
- 6 rectangular faces
- 8 vertices
- 12 edges
- Length l, width w, height h
- Volume: V = l×w×h
- Surface area: SA = 2(lw+lh+wh)
- Example: l=4, w=3, h=2 → V=24, SA=2(12+8+6)=52

Special cases:
- A cube is a rectangular prism with l=w=h
- A square prism has square bases"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Spheres and Cylinders{note:"Curved 3D shapes.

Sphere:
- All points equidistant from center
- Radius r
- Volume: V = (4/3)πr³
- Surface area: SA = 4πr²
- Example: r=3 → V=36π≈113.1, SA=36π≈113.1

Cylinder:
- Two circular bases connected by a curved surface
- Radius r, height h
- Volume: V = πr²h
- Surface area: SA = 2πr² + 2πrh (two bases + curved)
- Example: r=3, h=5 → V=45π≈141.3, SA=18π+30π=48π≈150.8

Half-sphere:
- Volume = (2/3)πr³
- Total surface area = 3πr² (base + curved)

Hollow cylinder:
- Volume = πh(R² - r²) (outer radius R, inner r)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Cones and Pyramids{note:"Shapes that taper to a point.

Cone:
- Circular base tapering to a point (apex)
- Radius r, height h, slant height l
- l = √(r² + h²)
- Volume: V = (1/3)πr²h
- Surface area: SA = πr² + πrl (base + curved)
- Example: r=3, h=4 → l=5, V=12π≈37.7, SA=9π+15π=24π≈75.4

Pyramid:
- Polygon base with triangular faces meeting at apex
- Volume: V = (1/3)×base area×height
- Square pyramid: base area = s² → V = (1/3)s²h
- Triangular pyramid: base area = (1/2)bh

Frustum:
- Cone or pyramid with the top cut off
- Truncated shape"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Platonic Solids{note:"Five regular 3D shapes.

Tetrahedron:
- 4 triangular faces
- 4 vertices
- 6 edges
- The simplest Platonic solid

Cube (Hexahedron):
- 6 square faces
- 8 vertices
- 12 edges
- Most common Platonic solid

Octahedron:
- 8 triangular faces
- 6 vertices
- 12 edges
- Two pyramids base-to-base

Dodecahedron:
- 12 pentagonal faces
- 20 vertices
- 30 edges
- The most complex (many faces)

Icosahedron:
- 20 triangular faces
- 12 vertices
- 30 edges
- The most face-rich

Properties:
- All faces are congruent regular polygons
- Same number of faces meet at each vertex
- Only five exist"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Applications of 3D Shapes{note:"Real-world applications.

Packaging:
- Boxes: rectangular prisms
- Cans: cylinders
- Balls: spheres
- Cones: party hats, traffic cones

Architecture:
- Pyramids: ancient tombs, modern buildings
- Domes: spheres or half-spheres
- Towers: cylinders or prisms

Engineering:
- Tanks: cylinders and spheres
- Pipes: hollow cylinders
- Gears: cylindrical with teeth

Everyday objects:
- Dice: cubes
- Balls: spheres
- Pencils: cylinders with cone tips
- Buildings: prisms with pyramid roofs

Storage:
- Volume determines capacity
- Surface area determines material needed
- Shape affects strength and stability"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[6]Perimeter{note:"Perimeter is the total distance around a polygon. For regular shapes: rectangle P=2(l+w), square P=4s, triangle P=a+b+c, circle C=2πr. Perimeter is used to determine fencing, framing, border lengths, and many real-world measurement tasks."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing perimeter calculations for various shapes."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Shapes{note:"Perimeter formulas for common shapes.

Rectangle:
- P = 2(l + w)
- Example: l=12, w=8 → P=2(12+8)=40

Square:
- P = 4s
- Example: s=7 → P=28

Triangle:
- P = a + b + c
- Example: 3,4,5 triangle → P=12

Equilateral triangle:
- P = 3s
- Example: s=6 → P=18

Circle:
- C = 2πr = πd
- Example: r=5 → C=2π×5≈31.4

Regular polygon:
- P = n × s (n sides, s side length)
- Hexagon side 4 → P=24
- Octagon side 3 → P=24"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Irregular Shapes{note:"Add all side lengths for any polygon.

Examples:
- Sides: 5, 7, 8, 6, 4 → P=30
- Sides: 3, 5, 4, 6 → P=18
- Sides: 2.5, 3.5, 4.5, 5.5 → P=16

Composite shapes:
- Add all outer side lengths
- For a rectangle with a semicircle attached:
  - Rectangle: 10×6
  - Semicircle with diameter 6
  - P = 10 + 6 + 10 + (π×3) = 26 + 9.42 = 35.42

Complex shapes:
- Break into simpler shapes
- Add the outer edges (not internal lines)
- Use subtraction for holes"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Real-World Perimeter Applications{note:"Perimeter in everyday situations.

Fencing:
- A yard 20ft×15ft → P=70ft
- Need 70 feet of fencing
- Cost: 70 × $15 = $1050

Picture framing:
- Picture 8in×10in → P=36in
- Need 36 inches of framing
- Cost: 36 × $2 = $72

Baseboards:
- Room 12ft×15ft → P=54ft
- Need 54 feet of baseboard
- Cost: 54 × $4 = $216

Running track:
- Track with two straight sections and two semicircles
- Straight: 100m each → 200m
- Semicircles: radius 35m → circumference = 2π×35 = 220m
- Total track = 200 + 220 = 420m

Gardens:
- Border of a circular garden: r=5m → C=31.4m
- Need 31.4m of edging"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]Area{note:"Area measures the amount of surface inside a 2D shape. Key formulas: rectangle A=l×w, square A=s², triangle A=(1/2)bh, circle A=πr², trapezoid A=(1/2)(b₁+b₂)h, parallelogram A=bh, regular polygon A=(1/2)ap (apothem×perimeter). Area is used in flooring, painting, land measurement, and countless real-world applications."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing area calculations for various shapes."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Shape Areas{note:"Area formulas for common shapes.

Rectangle:
- A = l × w
- Example: l=12, w=8 → A=96

Square:
- A = s²
- Example: s=7 → A=49

Triangle:
- A = (1/2)bh
- Example: b=10, h=6 → A=30
- Right triangle: A = (1/2)ab

Circle:
- A = πr²
- Example: r=5 → A=π×25≈78.54

Parallelogram:
- A = bh
- Example: b=8, h=5 → A=40

Trapezoid:
- A = (1/2)(b₁+b₂)h
- Example: b₁=8, b₂=12, h=5 → A=50

Rhombus:
- A = (1/2)d₁d₂
- Example: d₁=10, d₂=8 → A=40"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Heron's Formula for Triangles{note:"Area of any triangle from its sides.

Formula:
- s = (a+b+c)/2 (semi-perimeter)
- Area = √(s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c))

Examples:
- Triangle sides 3,4,5:
  s = (3+4+5)/2 = 6
  A = √(6×3×2×1) = √36 = 6

- Triangle sides 5,6,7:
  s = (5+6+7)/2 = 9
  A = √(9×4×3×2) = √216 ≈ 14.7

- Triangle sides 8,15,17:
  s = (8+15+17)/2 = 20
  A = √(20×12×5×3) = √3600 = 60

This formula is useful when height is not known."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Composite Shape Areas{note:"Break complex shapes into simpler parts.

Example 1: Rectangle with semicircle on top
- Rectangle: 10×6 = 60
- Semicircle: radius=3 → area = ½π×9 = 14.14
- Total area = 60 + 14.14 = 74.14

Example 2: L-shaped area
- Break into two rectangles
- Rectangle A: 8×4 = 32
- Rectangle B: 6×3 = 18
- Total area = 50

Example 3: Triangle with rectangle
- Rectangle base: 6×4 = 24
- Triangle on top: (1/2)×6×3 = 9
- Total area = 33

Example 4: Circle with square hole
- Outer circle: radius 5 → 78.54
- Inner square: 6×6 = 36
- Shaded area = 78.54 - 36 = 42.54"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Regular Polygon Areas{note:"Area of regular polygons using apothem.

Formula:
- Area = (1/2) × apothem × perimeter
- A = (1/2)ap

Examples:
- Regular hexagon: side=6, apothem=5.2
  Perimeter = 6×6 = 36
  Area = (1/2)×5.2×36 = 93.6

- Regular pentagon: side=4, apothem=2.75
  Perimeter = 5×4 = 20
  Area = (1/2)×2.75×20 = 27.5

- Square: side=5, apothem=2.5
  Perimeter = 20
  Area = (1/2)×2.5×20 = 25 (which is 5²)

Formula for area of regular polygon from side:
- Area = (n×s²)/(4×tan(π/n))
- For hexagon (n=6, s=6):
  Area = (6×36)/(4×tan30°) = 216/(4×0.577) = 216/2.309 = 93.53"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Surface Area{note:"Surface area is the total area of all faces of a 3D shape.

Cube:
- SA = 6s²
- Example: s=3 → SA=54

Rectangular prism:
- SA = 2(lw + lh + wh)
- Example: l=4, w=3, h=2 → SA=2(12+8+6)=52

Sphere:
- SA = 4πr²
- Example: r=3 → SA=36π≈113.1

Cylinder:
- SA = 2πr² + 2πrh
- Example: r=3, h=5 → SA=18π+30π=48π≈150.8

Cone:
- SA = πr² + πrl (l = slant height)
- Example: r=3, l=5 → SA=9π+15π=24π≈75.4

Pyramid:
- SA = base area + (1/2)×perimeter×slant height
- Square pyramid: s=4, slant=5
- Base area=16, lateral area=(1/2)×16×5=40
- SA=56"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]Volume{note:"Volume measures the amount of space occupied by a 3D object. Key formulas: cube V=s³, rectangular prism V=l×w×h, cylinder V=πr²h, cone V=(1/3)πr²h, sphere V=(4/3)πr³, pyramid V=(1/3)×base area×h. Volume is used in capacities, fluid measurements, packaging, and construction."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing volume calculations for various 3D shapes."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Shape Volumes{note:"Volume formulas for common 3D shapes.

Cube:
- V = s³
- Example: s=5 → V=125

Rectangular prism:
- V = l×w×h
- Example: l=4, w=3, h=2 → V=24

Cylinder:
- V = πr²h
- Example: r=3, h=5 → V=45π≈141.37

Cone:
- V = (1/3)πr²h
- Example: r=3, h=4 → V=12π≈37.7

Sphere:
- V = (4/3)πr³
- Example: r=3 → V=36π≈113.1

Pyramid:
- V = (1/3)×base area×height
- Example: square base s=4, h=6 → V=32

Triangular prism:
- V = base area × height
- Example: base area=12, h=8 → V=96"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Composite Shape Volumes{note:"Break complex 3D shapes into simpler parts.

Example 1: Cylinder with cone on top
- Cylinder: r=3, h=5 → V=45π
- Cone: r=3, h=3 → V=9π
- Total = 54π ≈ 169.65

Example 2: Prism with pyramid on top
- Prism: base area=16, h=4 → V=64
- Pyramid: base area=16, h=3 → V=16
- Total = 80

Example 3: Cylinder with hemispherical end
- Cylinder: r=2, h=6 → V=24π
- Hemisphere: r=2 → V=(2/3)π×8 = (16/3)π ≈ 16.76
- Total = (88/3)π ≈ 92.15

Example 4: Hollow cylinder (pipe)
- Outer radius R=3, inner r=2, h=10
- V = πh(R² - r²) = π×10×(9-4) = 50π"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Capacity Applications{note:"Volume as capacity for fluids.

Fish tank:
- 20cm×15cm×10cm → V=3000cm³ = 3 liters
- 1 cm³ = 1 mL
- 1000 cm³ = 1 L

Swimming pool:
- 10m×5m×2m → V=100m³
- 1 m³ = 1000 L
- Capacity = 100,000 L

Cylindrical tank:
- r=0.5m, h=2m → V=π×0.25×2 = 1.57m³
- Capacity = 1570 L

Grain silo:
- Cylinder with cone top
- Cylinder: r=3m, h=10m → V=90π m³
- Cone: r=3m, h=2m → V=6π m³
- Total = 96π ≈ 301.6 m³

Fuel tank:
- Cylindrical with hemispherical ends
- Cylinder: r=0.8m, h=4m → V=2.56π m³
- Two hemispheres: 2×(2/3)πr³ = (4/3)π×0.512 = 0.683π
- Total = 3.243π ≈ 10.19 m³ = 10,190 L"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Real-World Volume Applications{note:"Volume in everyday situations.

Packaging:
- Box volume determines shipping cost
- A box 30cm×20cm×15cm = 9000cm³
- For shipping, volume often converted to kg

Concrete:
- Slab 5m×4m×0.15m = 3m³
- Need 3 cubic meters of concrete
- 1 m³ ≈ 2400 kg (weight of concrete)

Storage:
- A storage unit 3m×2m×2.5m = 15m³
- Capacity for boxes and furniture

Construction:
- Excavation volume: length×width×depth
- Backfilling: volume of material needed

Weight from volume:
- Water: 1 L = 1 kg
- Concrete: 1 m³ ≈ 2400 kg
- Steel: 1 m³ ≈ 7850 kg
- Wood: 1 m³ ≈ 500-800 kg

Air in a room:
- Room 4m×5m×3m = 60m³
- This is the volume of air in the room"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[5]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Proof: Volume of a Sphere{note:"Derivation of the sphere volume formula.

Step 1: Consider a sphere of radius R.
Step 2: Use the method of disks (calculus).
Step 3: At height y from the center, the cross-section is a circle with radius:
r = √(R² - y²)
Step 4: The area of this disk is:
A(y) = πr² = π(R² - y²)
Step 5: Integrate from y = -R to y = R:
V = ∫_{-R}^{R} π(R² - y²) dy
Step 6: Evaluate the integral:
V = π [R²y - y³/3]_{-R}^{R}
V = π [(R³ - R³/3) - (-R³ + R³/3)]
V = π [(2R³/3) - (-2R³/3)]
V = π (4R³/3)
Step 7: Therefore:
V = (4/3)πR³

This is the formula for the volume of a sphere.

Alternative proof using Archimedes:
- A sphere fits exactly inside a cylinder with height 2R
- The volume of the cylinder is πR²(2R) = 2πR³
- The sphere is 2/3 of the cylinder volume
- V = (2/3)×2πR³ = (4/3)πR³"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]Measurement{note:"Measurement assigns numbers to physical quantities using standard units. It is essential for science, engineering, construction, commerce, and everyday life."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[1]Units of Length{note:"Length units measure distance. Metric: millimeter (mm), centimeter (cm), meter (m), kilometer (km). Imperial: inch (in), foot (ft), yard (yd), mile (mi). Understanding units and conversions is essential for construction, travel, and scientific measurement."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing length units and conversions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Metric Length Conversions{note:"Conversions within the metric system.

Basic units:
- 1 km = 1000 m
- 1 m = 100 cm
- 1 cm = 10 mm

Examples:
- 2.5 km = 2500 m
- 1.75 km = 1750 m
- 3.2 m = 320 cm
- 150 cm = 1.5 m
- 45 cm = 450 mm
- 345 mm = 34.5 cm
- 5678 m = 5.678 km

Prefix meanings:
- kilo: 1000
- hecto: 100
- deca: 10
- base: 1 (m, g, L)
- deci: 0.1
- centi: 0.01
- milli: 0.001

Moving the decimal:
- km → m: multiply by 1000 (move decimal 3 right)
- m → cm: multiply by 100 (move decimal 2 right)
- cm → mm: multiply by 10 (move decimal 1 right)
- mm → cm: divide by 10 (move decimal 1 left)
- cm → m: divide by 100 (move decimal 2 left)
- m → km: divide by 1000 (move decimal 3 left)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Imperial Length Conversions{note:"Conversions within the imperial system.

Basic units:
- 1 ft = 12 in
- 1 yd = 3 ft = 36 in
- 1 mi = 5280 ft

Examples:
- 5 ft 3 in = (5×12+3) = 63 in
- 2.5 yd = 2.5×3 = 7.5 ft = 90 in
- 1.5 mi = 1.5×5280 = 7920 ft
- 36 inches = 3 feet = 1 yard
- 72 inches = 6 feet = 2 yards

Feet to inches:
- multiply by 12
- 4 ft = 48 in
- 3.5 ft = 42 in

Inches to feet:
- divide by 12
- 60 in = 5 ft
- 42 in = 3.5 ft

Yards to feet:
- multiply by 3
- 5 yd = 15 ft

Miles to feet:
- multiply by 5280
- 2 mi = 10,560 ft"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Metric to Imperial Length{note:"Converting between metric and imperial units.

- 1 ft ≈ 0.3048 m
- 1 yd ≈ 0.9144 m
- 1 mi ≈ 1.60934 km
- 1 cm ≈ 0.3937 in
- 1 m ≈ 3.28084 ft
- 1 km ≈ 0.621371 mi

- 50 cm = 50×0.3937 = 19.685 in
- 2 m = 2×3.28084 = 6.5617 ft

Quick approximations:
- 1 m ≈ 3.28 ft (or 3 ft 3.4 in)
- 1 km ≈ 0.62 mi (5 km ≈ 3.1 mi)
- 1 inch ≈ 2.5 cm
- 1 ft ≈ 30 cm"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]Units of Time{note:"Time units measure duration: second (s), minute (min), hour (h), day, week, month, year. Understanding time is essential for scheduling, science, and everyday life."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing time conversions and calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Time Conversions{note:"Converting between time units.

- 1 min = 60 s
- 1 h = 60 min = 3600 s
- 1 day = 24 h = 1440 min = 86,400 s
- 1 week = 7 days
- 1 month ≈ 30 days (varies)
- 1 year = 365 days (common) or 365.25 days (including leap years)

- 2.5 h = 150 min = 9000 s

- 3 days = 72 h = 4320 min = 259,200 s
- 1.5 weeks = 10.5 days

- 3600 s = 1 hour
- 48 h = 2 days
- 72 h = 3 days

- Days to hours: multiply by 24

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Time Calculations{note:"Adding and subtracting time.

Adding time:
- 2:30 PM to 5:15 PM = 2h 45min
- 10:15 AM + 3h 45min = 2:00 PM
  (10:15 + 3h = 1:15, +45min = 2:00)
- 4:20 PM + 2h 30min = 6:50 PM

Subtracting time:
- 5:15 PM - 2:30 PM = 2h 45min
- 11:45 AM - 8:30 AM = 3h 15min
- 2:00 AM - 10:00 PM = 4 hours (next day)

Elapsed time:
- From 8:30 AM to 12:45 PM = 4h 15min
- From 9:00 AM to 5:30 PM = 8h 30min
- From 11:15 AM to 2:00 PM = 2h 45min

Time zones:
- UTC+5 to UTC-5 = 10 hour difference
- 9:00 AM UTC+5 = 11:00 PM UTC-5 (previous day)
- Crossing date line: add/subtract a day

Decimal time:
- 2.5 hours = 2h 30min
- 3.25 hours = 3h 15min
- 1.75 hours = 1h 45min"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Time in Real Life{note:"Common time measurements.

Days and weeks:
- 1 week = 7 days
- 2 weeks = 14 days (fortnight)
- 4 weeks ≈ 1 month
- 1 year = 52 weeks + 1 day

Months:
- January: 31 days
- February: 28 days (29 in leap year)
- March: 31 days
- April: 30 days
- May: 31 days
- June: 30 days
- July: 31 days
- August: 31 days
- September: 30 days
- October: 31 days
- November: 30 days
- December: 31 days

Years:
- 1 year = 12 months
- 1 decade = 10 years
- 1 century = 100 years
- 1 millennium = 1000 years

Leap year:
- Divisible by 4 (except centuries not divisible by 400)
- 2024, 2028, 2032 are leap years
- 1900 was not a leap year
- 2000 was a leap year"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Speed and Time{note:"Relationship between speed, distance, and time.

Formula:
- Distance = Speed × Time
- Speed = Distance / Time
- Time = Distance / Speed

Examples:
- Travel 100 km at 50 km/h: time = 100/50 = 2 hours
- Travel at 60 mph for 3 hours: distance = 60×3 = 180 miles
- Run 5 km in 30 minutes: speed = 5/0.5 = 10 km/h

Unit conversions:
- 1 km/h = 1000 m/3600 s ≈ 0.2778 m/s
- 1 m/s = 3.6 km/h
- 60 mph ≈ 96.6 km/h
- 100 km/h ≈ 62.1 mph

Average speed:
- Total distance / total time
- 240 km in 4 hours = 60 km/h
- 300 miles in 5 hours = 60 mph

Time zones:
- Earth rotates 360° in 24 hours
- Each hour = 15° of longitude
- UTC: Coordinated Universal Time"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[5]Unit Conversion{note:"Unit conversion changes measurement from one unit to another using conversion factors. This is essential across all fields of science, engineering, and daily life."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing unit conversion with conversion factors."}

- 6 ft to m: 6×0.3048 = 1.8288 m

- 50 cm to in: 50×0.3937 = 19.685 in"}

- 1 oz ≈ 28.35 g
- 1 g ≈ 0.035 oz"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Time Conversions{note:"Converting time units.

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Conversion Factor Method{note:"Using conversion factors to convert units.

Method:
- Multiply by a fraction equal to 1
- The fraction has the old unit in denominator and new unit in numerator

Example 1: Convert 5 miles to kilometers
- 1 mi = 1.609 km
- 5 mi × (1.609 km / 1 mi) = 8.045 km

Example 2: Convert 100 km/h to m/s
- 1 km = 1000 m, 1 h = 3600 s
- 100 km/h × (1000 m / 1 km) × (1 h / 3600 s)
- = 100 × 1000 / 3600 = 27.78 m/s

Example 3: Convert 60 mph to km/h
- 1 mi = 1.609 km
- 60 mi/h × (1.609 km / 1 mi) = 96.54 km/h

Example 4: Convert 2.5 kg to oz
- 1 kg = 2.20462 lb, 1 lb = 16 oz
- 2.5 kg × (2.20462 lb / 1 kg) × (16 oz / 1 lb)
- = 2.5 × 2.20462 × 16 = 88.1848 oz

Example 5: Convert 3 L to gal
- 1 L = 0.264172 gal
- 3 L × (0.264172 gal / 1 L) = 0.792516 gal"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[6]Metric System{note:"The metric system (SI units) is a decimal-based system used worldwide. Base units: meter (m) for length, kilogram (kg) for mass, second (s) for time, ampere (A) for current, kelvin (K) for temperature, mole (mol) for amount, candela (cd) for luminous intensity. Prefixes: kilo (10³), centi (10⁻²), milli (10⁻³), micro (10⁻⁶), mega (10⁶), giga (10⁹). The metric system's simplicity makes it preferred in science and most countries."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing metric prefixes and conversions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Metric Prefixes{note:"The prefixes of the metric system.

Large prefixes:
- kilo (k) = 10³ = 1000
- mega (M) = 10⁶ = 1,000,000
- giga (G) = 10⁹ = 1,000,000,000
- tera (T) = 10¹² = 1,000,000,000,000
- peta (P) = 10¹⁵
- exa (E) = 10¹⁸

Base units:
- meter (m) for length
- gram (g) for mass
- liter (L) for volume
- second (s) for time

Small prefixes:
- deci (d) = 10⁻¹ = 0.1
- centi (c) = 10⁻² = 0.01
- milli (m) = 10⁻³ = 0.001
- micro (μ) = 10⁻⁶ = 0.000001
- nano (n) = 10⁻⁹ = 0.000000001
- pico (p) = 10⁻¹²

Examples:
- 5 km = 5000 m (kilo = 1000)
- 3 cm = 0.03 m (centi = 0.01)
- 2 mg = 0.002 g (milli = 0.001)
- 4 μL = 0.000004 L (micro = 10⁻⁶)
- 3 GHz = 3,000,000,000 Hz (giga = 10⁹)
- 2 MB = 2,000,000 bytes (mega = 10⁶)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Metric System in Science{note:"SI units in physics and science.

Base SI units:
- Length: meter (m)
- Mass: kilogram (kg)
- Time: second (s)
- Electric current: ampere (A)
- Temperature: kelvin (K)
- Amount of substance: mole (mol)
- Luminous intensity: candela (cd)

Derived SI units:
- Force: newton (N) = kg·m/s²
- Energy: joule (J) = N·m = kg·m²/s²
- Power: watt (W) = J/s = kg·m²/s³
- Pressure: pascal (Pa) = N/m² = kg/(m·s²)
- Frequency: hertz (Hz) = s⁻¹
- Voltage: volt (V) = W/A = kg·m²/(s³·A)

Common SI values:
- Speed of light: 3.00×10⁸ m/s
- Acceleration due to gravity: 9.8 m/s²
- Standard pressure: 101,325 Pa
- 1 J = energy to lift 1 kg by 10 cm
- 100 W = 100 J/s
- 1 kg object weighs about 9.8 N"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Metric Conversions{note:"Converting between metric prefixes.

Length:
- 1 km = 1000 m
- 1 m = 100 cm = 1000 mm
- 1 cm = 10 mm
- 1 μm = 0.000001 m = 10⁻⁶ m
- 1 nm = 0.000000001 m = 10⁻⁹ m

Mass:
- 1 kg = 1000 g
- 1 g = 1000 mg
- 1 mg = 1000 μg
- 1 t = 1000 kg = 1,000,000 g

Volume:
- 1 L = 1000 mL = 1000 cm³
- 1 m³ = 1000 L = 1,000,000 mL
- 1 mL = 1 cm³

Examples:
- 2.5 km = 2500 m = 250,000 cm
- 3.2 kg = 3200 g = 3,200,000 mg
- 4.5 L = 4500 mL = 4500 cm³
- 750 mL = 0.75 L
- 150 cm = 1.5 m
- 2500 mg = 2.5 g"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[7]Imperial System{note:"The imperial system (US customary units) is used primarily in the United States. Units include inches, feet, yards, miles for length; ounces, pounds, tons for weight; fluid ounces, cups, pints, quarts, gallons for volume. While less systematic than metric, it remains common in US commerce, construction, and daily life."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Imperial Cooking Measurements{note:"Common cooking measurements in imperial.

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Imperial vs Metric Conversion{note:"Converting between imperial and metric units.

- 1 in = 2.54 cm
- 1 ft = 30.48 cm = 0.3048 m
- 1 yd = 0.9144 m
- 1 mi = 1.609 km

Mass:
- 1 oz = 28.3495 g
- 1 lb = 0.453592 kg
- 1 ton = 907.185 kg

- 1 fl oz = 29.5735 mL

- 1 pt = 473.176 mL
- 1 qt = 946.353 mL
- 1 gal = 3.78541 L

- °F = (°C × 9/5) + 32
- °C = (°F - 32) × 5/9

Examples:
- 68°F = 20°C (room temp)
- 98.6°F = 37°C (body temp)
- 212°F = 100°C (boiling point)
- 32°F = 0°C (freezing point)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]SI Units{note:"SI units (Système International) are the modern metric system officially used in science and most countries. The seven base units: meter (m), kilogram (kg), second (s), ampere (A), kelvin (K), mole (mol), candela (cd). Derived units include newton (N), joule (J), watt (W), pascal (Pa), hertz (Hz), and many others. SI is essential for scientific communication and standardization."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing SI base and derived units."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Base SI Units{note:"The seven fundamental SI units.

Length:
- Unit: meter (m)
- Definition: distance light travels in 1/299,792,458 seconds

Mass:
- Unit: kilogram (kg)
- Definition: defined by Planck's constant (h)

Time:
- Unit: second (s)
- Definition: duration of 9,192,631,770 periods of cesium-133 radiation

Electric current:
- Unit: ampere (A)
- Definition: defined by elementary charge (e)

Temperature:
- Unit: kelvin (K)
- Definition: defined by Boltzmann constant (k)

Amount of substance:
- Unit: mole (mol)
- Definition: 6.02214076×10²³ particles

Luminous intensity:
- Unit: candela (cd)
- Definition: defined by luminous efficacy of 540 THz radiation"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Derived SI Units{note:"Derived units from the base SI units.

Mechanical:
- Newton (N): kg·m/s² (force)
- Joule (J): kg·m²/s² (energy, work)
- Watt (W): kg·m²/s³ (power)
- Pascal (Pa): kg/(m·s²) (pressure)
- Hertz (Hz): s⁻¹ (frequency)

Electrical:
- Volt (V): kg·m²/(s³·A) (electric potential)
- Ohm (Ω): kg·m²/(s³·A²) (resistance)
- Farad (F): s⁴·A²/(kg·m²) (capacitance)
- Siemens (S): s³·A²/(kg·m²) (conductance)

Magnetic:
- Tesla (T): kg/(s²·A) (magnetic flux density)
- Weber (Wb): kg·m²/(s²·A) (magnetic flux)

Other:
- Celsius (°C): K - 273.15
- Liter (L): 0.001 m³
- Byte (B): 8 bits"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]SI Prefixes in Use{note:"Common SI prefixes in science and everyday life.

- 1 km = 1000 m

- 1 nm = 10⁻⁹ m
