Data:
- 1 kB = 1000 bytes (or 1024)
- 1 MB = 1,000,000 bytes
- 1 GB = 1,000,000,000 bytes
- 1 TB = 1,000,000,000,000 bytes"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]SI in Science{note:"SI units in scientific contexts.

Physics constants:
- Speed of light: c = 2.99792458×10⁸ m/s
- Planck's constant: h = 6.62607015×10⁻³⁴ J·s
- Elementary charge: e = 1.602176634×10⁻¹⁹ C
- Boltzmann constant: k = 1.380649×10⁻²³ J/K

Astronomy:
- Astronomical unit: 1 AU = 1.496×10¹¹ m
- Light year: 1 ly = 9.461×10¹⁵ m
- Parsec: 1 pc = 3.086×10¹⁶ m

Chemistry:
- Mole: 6.022×10²³ particles
- Avogadro's number: N_A = 6.02214076×10²³ mol⁻¹
- Molar mass: kg/mol or g/mol

Medicine:
- Pressure: 1 atm = 101,325 Pa = 760 mmHg
- Concentration: mol/L (molarity)
- Dose: mg/kg (milligrams per kilogram)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]Significant Figures{note:"Significant figures (sig figs) indicate the precision of a measurement. Rules: non-zero digits are significant, zeros between non-zero digits are significant, trailing zeros after decimal are significant, leading zeros are not. Significant figures are essential in scientific measurements to show the reliability of data and avoid false precision."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing significant figures and operations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Counting Significant Figures{note:"Rules for counting significant figures.

Non-zero digits:
- Always significant
- 3.45 has 3 sig figs
- 2.7 has 2 sig figs
- 5.678 has 4 sig figs

Zeros between non-zero digits:
- Always significant
- 304 has 3 sig figs
- 1002 has 4 sig figs
- 5.003 has 4 sig figs

Zeros after decimal (trailing):
- Significant
- 100.0 has 4 sig figs
- 0.0500 has 3 sig figs (5 and trailing zeros)
- 3.500 has 4 sig figs

Leading zeros:
- Not significant
- 0.0045 has 2 sig figs (4 and 5)
- 0.00056 has 2 sig figs
- 0.0250 has 3 sig figs (2,5, and trailing zero)

Zeros without decimal (ambiguous):
- 100 has 1 sig fig (ambiguous without decimal)
- 100. has 3 sig figs (with decimal point)
- 1.0×10² has 2 sig figs (scientific notation)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Addition and Subtraction Sig Figs{note:"Rules for addition and subtraction with significant figures.

Rule: The result has the same number of decimal places as the least precise measurement.

Examples:
- 3.45 + 2.7 = 6.15 → 6.2 (one decimal place)
  3.45 (2 decimal), 2.7 (1 decimal) → round to 1 decimal
- 12.34 - 5.6 = 6.74 → 6.7 (one decimal place)
  12.34 (2 decimal), 5.6 (1 decimal) → round to 1 decimal
- 100.0 - 3.45 = 96.55 → 96.6 (one decimal place)
  100.0 (1 decimal), 3.45 (2 decimal) → round to 1 decimal
- 0.5 + 0.25 = 0.75 → 0.8 (one decimal place)
  0.5 (1 decimal), 0.25 (2 decimal) → round to 1 decimal
- 10.0 + 3.75 = 13.75 → 13.8 (one decimal place)

Rounding:
- 6.15 → 6.2 (5 rounds up)
- 6.74 → 6.7 (4 rounds down)
- 96.55 → 96.6 (5 rounds up)
- 0.75 → 0.8 (5 rounds up)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Multiplication and Division Sig Figs{note:"Rules for multiplication and division with significant figures.

Rule: The result has the same number of significant figures as the least precise measurement.

Examples:
- 3.45 × 2.7 = 9.315 → 9.3 (2 sig figs)
  3.45 (3 sig figs), 2.7 (2 sig figs) → 2 sig figs
- 12.34 × 5.6 = 69.104 → 69 (2 sig figs)
  12.34 (4 sig figs), 5.6 (2 sig figs) → 2 sig figs
- 100 ÷ 3.45 = 28.9855 → 29 (1 sig fig)
  100 (1 sig fig), 3.45 (3 sig figs) → 1 sig fig
- 2.5 × 4.0 = 10.0 → 10.0 (2 sig figs)
  2.5 (2 sig figs), 4.0 (2 sig figs) → 2 sig figs
- 3.5 × 2 = 7 (1 sig fig)
  3.5 (2 sig figs), 2 (1 sig fig) → 1 sig fig

Examples with scientific notation:
- (3.45×10³)(2.7×10²) = 9.315×10⁵ → 9.3×10⁵ (2 sig figs)
- (6.0×10⁵) ÷ (2.0×10³) = 3.0×10² (2 sig figs)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Rounding Rules{note:"Rules for rounding numbers to significant figures.

General rules:
- Look at the digit to the right of the last significant figure
- If the digit is 5 or greater, round up
- If the digit is 4 or less, round down

Examples:
- Round to 3 sig figs: 3.456 → 3.46
- Round to 2 sig figs: 3.456 → 3.5
- Round to 1 sig fig: 3.456 → 3
- Round to 2 sig figs: 0.00456 → 0.0046
- Round to 1 sig fig: 0.00456 → 0.005
- Round to 3 sig figs: 123.456 → 123
- Round to 4 sig figs: 123.456 → 123.5
- Round to 2 sig figs: 1000 → 1.0×10³
- Round to 3 sig figs: 1000 → 1.00×10³

Trailing zeros:
- 3.456 to 2 sig figs: 3.5
- 0.00456 to 2 sig figs: 0.0046
- 10.0 to 2 sig figs: 10.0
- 100 to 1 sig fig: 1×10²

When rounding 5:
- 3.45 to 2 sig figs: 3.5 (round up)
- 3.65 to 2 sig figs: 3.7 (round up)
- 3.35 to 2 sig figs: 3.4 (round up)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Significant Figures in Calculations{note:"Combining operations with significant figures.

Example: (3.456 × 2.7) + 5.8

Step 1: Multiplication
- 3.456 × 2.7 = 9.3312 → 9.3 (2 sig figs)

Step 2: Addition
- 9.3 + 5.8 = 15.1 → 15.1 (one decimal place)

Example: (100.0 - 3.45) × 2.5

Step 1: Subtraction
- 100.0 - 3.45 = 96.55 → 96.6 (one decimal place)

Step 2: Multiplication
- 96.6 × 2.5 = 241.5 → 2.4×10² (2 sig figs)

Example: (5.6 + 2.34) / 3.5

Step 1: Addition
- 5.6 + 2.34 = 7.94 → 7.9 (one decimal place)

Step 2: Division
- 7.9 / 3.5 = 2.257 → 2.3 (2 sig figs)

Rules for combined operations:
- Do operations in order
- Apply sig fig rules at each step
- Keep extra digits for intermediate steps to avoid rounding errors"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]Scientific Notation{note:"Scientific notation expresses numbers as a × 10^n where 1 ≤ a < 10. It simplifies very large and very small numbers, making them easier to read, write, and calculate. Essential in science, engineering, and astronomy for representing quantities like Avogadro's number (6.02×10²³) or the mass of an electron (9.11×10⁻³¹ kg)."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing scientific notation and conversions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Converting to Scientific Notation{note:"Write numbers in scientific notation.

Steps:
1. Move the decimal point to get a number between 1 and 10
2. Count how many places the decimal moved
3. If moved left, exponent is positive
4. If moved right, exponent is negative

Examples:
- 3,450,000 = 3.45×10⁶ (moved 6 left)
- 0.000456 = 4.56×10⁻⁴ (moved 4 right)
- 602,000,000,000,000,000,000,000 = 6.02×10²³
- 0.0000000000911 = 9.11×10⁻¹¹
- 1000 = 1.0×10³
- 0.01 = 1.0×10⁻²
- 1,000,000 = 1.0×10⁶
- 0.000001 = 1.0×10⁻⁶
- 345,000 = 3.45×10⁵
- 0.0000789 = 7.89×10⁻⁵

Rules:
- Large numbers: positive exponent
- Small numbers: negative exponent
- Number between 1 and 10: a (coefficient)
- 10^n: power of 10"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Converting from Scientific Notation{note:"Write scientific notation as standard numbers.

Steps:
- If exponent is positive, move decimal right
- If exponent is negative, move decimal left

Examples:
- 5.67×10⁵ = 567,000 (move decimal 5 right)
- 3.2×10⁻³ = 0.0032 (move decimal 3 left)
- 8.9×10⁷ = 89,000,000
- 4.56×10⁻⁶ = 0.00000456
- 1.0×10⁹ = 1,000,000,000
- 2.5×10⁻⁴ = 0.00025
- 9.0×10³ = 9000
- 7.8×10⁻² = 0.078
- 3.14×10¹ = 31.4
- 6.02×10²³ = 602,000,000,000,000,000,000,000

Rule:
- Positive exponent: number is large
- Negative exponent: number is small
- Add trailing zeros as needed"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Operations in Scientific Notation{note:"Perform calculations with numbers in scientific notation.

Multiplication:
- Multiply coefficients
- Add exponents
- Adjust if necessary

(3.2×10⁴) × (2.5×10³) = (3.2×2.5) × 10⁴⁺³ = 8.0×10⁷

(2.0×10⁶) × (1.5×10⁻²) = 3.0×10⁴

(4.5×10⁵) × (2.0×10³) = 9.0×10⁸

Division:
- Divide coefficients
- Subtract exponents

(6.0×10⁵) ÷ (2.0×10³) = (6.0/2.0) × 10⁵⁻³ = 3.0×10²

(8.0×10⁷) ÷ (4.0×10²) = 2.0×10⁵

(5.0×10⁻³) ÷ (2.5×10⁻⁵) = 2.0×10²

Addition/Subtraction:
- Must have same exponent
- Add or subtract coefficients

(4.5×10³) + (2.3×10⁴) = 0.45×10⁴ + 2.3×10⁴ = 2.75×10⁴

(2.5×10⁶) - (1.2×10⁵) = 2.5×10⁶ - 0.12×10⁶ = 2.38×10⁶

(3.4×10⁴) + (5.6×10³) = 3.4×10⁴ + 0.56×10⁴ = 3.96×10⁴"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Applications of Scientific Notation{note:"Scientific notation in science and everyday life.

Physics:
- Speed of light: 3.00×10⁸ m/s
- Mass of electron: 9.11×10⁻³¹ kg
- Charge of electron: 1.60×10⁻¹⁹ C
- Planck's constant: 6.626×10⁻³⁴ J·s
- Gravitational constant: 6.67×10⁻¹¹ N·m²/kg²

Astronomy:
- Distance to Sun: 1.496×10¹¹ m
- Mass of Earth: 5.97×10²⁴ kg
- Mass of Sun: 1.99×10³⁰ kg
- Radius of Earth: 6.37×10⁶ m
- Light year: 9.46×10¹⁵ m

Chemistry:
- Avogadro's number: 6.02×10²³ mol⁻¹
- Atomic mass unit: 1.66×10⁻²⁷ kg
- Bohr radius: 5.29×10⁻¹¹ m

Biology:
- DNA diameter: 2.5×10⁻⁹ m
- Virus size: 1×10⁻⁷ m
- Cell size: 1×10⁻⁵ m

Everyday:
- 1 million: 1×10⁶
- 1 billion: 1×10⁹
- 1 trillion: 1×10¹²
- 1 nanometer: 1×10⁻⁹ m"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[10]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Engineering Notation{note:"Engineering notation is a variant of scientific notation.

Rules:
- Exponent must be a multiple of 3
- 10³, 10⁶, 10⁹, 10¹² (positive)
- 10⁻³, 10⁻⁶, 10⁻⁹, 10⁻¹² (negative)

Examples:
- 3,450,000 = 3.45×10⁶ (engineering)
- 3,450,000 = 3.45×10⁶ (same as scientific)

- 0.000456 = 456×10⁻⁶ (engineering)
- 0.000456 = 4.56×10⁻⁴ (scientific)

- 602,000,000,000,000,000,000,000 = 602×10²¹ (engineering)
- 602,000,000,000,000,000,000,000 = 6.02×10²³ (scientific)

Common engineering prefixes:
- 10³: kilo (k)
- 10⁶: mega (M)
- 10⁹: giga (G)
- 10¹²: tera (T)
- 10⁻³: milli (m)
- 10⁻⁶: micro (μ)
- 10⁻⁹: nano (n)
- 10⁻¹²: pico (p)

Examples in engineering:
- 3.5×10⁶ Ω = 3.5 MΩ (megaohm)
- 2.2×10⁻⁹ F = 2.2 nF (nanofarad)
- 4.7×10³ V = 4.7 kV (kilovolt)
- 1.5×10⁻³ A = 1.5 mA (milliampere)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]Precision and Accuracy{note:"Precision refers to the repeatability of measurements (consistency). Accuracy refers to how close a measurement is to the true value. A measurement can be precise but inaccurate (consistent but wrong), or accurate but imprecise (correct average but inconsistent). Understanding both is essential for scientific experiments, quality control, and data analysis."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing precision and accuracy differences."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Target Analogy{note:"The target analogy illustrates precision and accuracy.

Case 1: Accurate and precise
- All shots in the bullseye
- Measurements cluster tightly around the true value
- Example: 100.1, 100.0, 99.9 (true value = 100)

Case 2: Precise but not accurate
- All shots close together but off-center
- Measurements consistent but wrong
- Example: 105.0, 105.1, 104.9 (true value = 100)
- Systematic error present (bias)

Case 3: Accurate but not precise
- Shots scattered but centered on bullseye
- Measurements average to correct value but vary widely
- Example: 95, 105, 97, 103 (true value = 100)
- Random error present

Case 4: Neither accurate nor precise
- Shots scattered and off-center
- Measurements inconsistent and wrong
- Example: 85, 115, 90, 110 (true value = 100)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Measurement Examples{note:"Examples of precision and accuracy in measurements.

True value = 100

Case 1: Precise and accurate
- Measurements: 101, 100, 99, 101, 100
- Average: 100.2
- Standard deviation: small
- Close to true value

Case 2: Precise but not accurate
- Measurements: 105, 106, 104, 105, 106
- Average: 105.2
- Standard deviation: small
- Far from true value (systematic error)

Case 3: Accurate but not precise
- Measurements: 95, 105, 97, 103, 104
- Average: 100.8
- Standard deviation: large
- Close to true value on average

Case 4: Neither precise nor accurate
- Measurements: 80, 120, 90, 130, 85
- Average: 101
- Standard deviation: very large
- Far from true value and inconsistent

Systematic error: consistent bias in one direction
Random error: unpredictable fluctuations
Human error: mistakes in reading or recording"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Reporting Precision{note:"How precision is indicated in measurements.

Number of decimal places:
- More decimal places = more precise
- 100.0g is more precise than 100g
- 100.00g is more precise than 100.0g

Significant figures:
- More sig figs = more precise
- 100.0 has 4 sig figs
- 100 has 1 sig fig (unless specified)

Uncertainty:
- Measurement ± uncertainty
- 5.6 cm ± 0.1 cm
- 25.00 g ± 0.01 g
- True value between 5.5 and 5.7 cm

Examples:
- A digital scale reading 100.0g vs 100.00g
  The second is more precise

- A clock accurate to 0.1 seconds
  More precise than one accurate to 1 second

- A thermometer reading 25.0°C
  More precise than 25°C

- A ruler with mm markings
  More precise than one with cm markings"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Precision in Calculations{note:"Precision propagates through calculations.

Multiplication/Division:
- Result has same sig figs as least precise
- 3.45 (3 sig figs) × 2.7 (2 sig figs) = 9.3 (2 sig figs)

Addition/Subtraction:
- Result has same decimal places as least precise
- 3.45 (2 decimal) + 2.7 (1 decimal) = 6.2 (1 decimal)

Rounding in multi-step:
- Keep extra digits in intermediate steps
- Round only the final answer

Example:
- (3.456 × 2.7) + 5.8
- Keep 3.456×2.7 = 9.3312 (not rounded yet)
- 9.3312 + 5.8 = 15.1312
- Final: 15.1 (one decimal place)

Reporting precision:
- Don't report more digits than justified
- 12.34 with uncertainty 0.5 should be 12.3 ± 0.5
- 12.34 with uncertainty 0.05 should be 12.34 ± 0.05"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[11]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Improving Accuracy and Precision{note:"Methods to improve measurements.

Improving accuracy:
- Calibrate instruments
- Use standards for comparison
- Correct for systematic errors
- Use more accurate instruments
- Follow proper procedures

Improving precision:
- Take multiple measurements
- Average readings
- Use instruments with finer divisions
- Reduce random errors
- Control environmental factors

Quality control:
- Check instruments regularly
- Use control samples
- Track measurements over time
- Identify sources of error

Reducing error:
- Systematic error: recalibrate
- Random error: take more data
- Human error: train operators
- Environmental: control conditions

Examples:
- A scale that reads 0.5g high
  Add -0.5g to all readings

- A ruler with worn markings
  Replace the ruler

- Temperature fluctuations affect measurements
  Control the temperature"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]Error and Uncertainty{note:"Error is the difference between measured and true values. Uncertainty expresses the range in which the true value is expected to lie. Sources: systematic errors (bias), random errors (unpredictable), and human error. Understanding error and uncertainty is essential for interpreting experimental results and making reliable measurements."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing error and uncertainty calculations."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Absolute and Relative Error{note:"Calculating measurement error.

Absolute error:
- |measured - true|
- Example: true = 100 cm, measured = 102 cm
  Absolute error = |102 - 100| = 2 cm

Relative error:
- (absolute error / true value) × 100%
- Example: 2/100 × 100% = 2%

Percent error:
- Same as relative error
- (|measured - true| / true) × 100%

Examples:
- True length 50 cm, measured 48 cm
  Absolute error = 2 cm
  Relative error = 2/50 × 100% = 4%

- True mass 200 g, measured 205 g
  Absolute error = 5 g
  Relative error = 5/200 × 100% = 2.5%

- True time 10.0 s, measured 9.8 s
  Absolute error = 0.2 s
  Relative error = 0.2/10 × 100% = 2%

- True value 5.0 mL, measured 5.2 mL
  Absolute error = 0.2 mL
  Relative error = 0.2/5 × 100% = 4%"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Uncertainty Range{note:"Expressing measurement uncertainty.

Uncertainty notation:
- Measurement ± uncertainty
- 5.6 cm ± 0.1 cm
- True value between 5.5 and 5.7 cm

Examples:
- Scale with ±0.5g uncertainty, reads 100.0g
  True value between 99.5g and 100.5g

- Ruler with ±0.5mm uncertainty, reads 25.0cm
  True value between 24.95cm and 25.05cm

- Thermometer with ±0.2°C, reads 37.0°C
  True value between 36.8°C and 37.2°C

- Timer with ±0.01s, reads 12.34s
  True value between 12.33s and 12.35s

Rounding to uncertainty:
- 12.34 ± 0.05 (correct)
- 12.34 ± 0.5 → 12.3 ± 0.5 (round to match uncertainty)
- 12.34 ± 0.005 → 12.340 ± 0.005

Propagation of uncertainty:
- Addition/subtraction: add uncertainties
- Multiplication/division: add relative uncertainties"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Types of Error{note:"Different sources of measurement error.

Systematic error (bias):
- Consistent error in one direction
- Caused by faulty calibration, zero offset, etc.
- Can be corrected with calibration
- Example: A scale always reads 0.5g too high

Random error (precision):
- Unpredictable fluctuations
- Caused by environmental variations, reading error, etc.
- Reduced by taking more measurements
- Example: Slight variations in reading the same value

Human error (mistakes):
- Errors in reading, recording, calculation
- Can be reduced by careful technique and checking
- Example: Misreading a scale by 1 mm

Gross error:
- Large, obvious error
- Usually due to a mistake
- Should be discarded

Examples:
- Systematic: A ruler with a damaged zero mark
- Random: Temperature fluctuations during an experiment
- Human: Recording 12.34 as 12.43
- Gross: Using wrong units (cm instead of m)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Calculating Percent Error{note:"Percent error calculations.

Formula:
- Percent error = (|measured - true| / true) × 100%

Examples:
- Measured 5.0 mL, true is 5.2 mL
  Error = -0.2 mL
  Percent error = |0.2|/5.2 × 100% = 3.85%

- Measured 12.3 cm, true is 12.0 cm
  Error = 0.3 cm
  Percent error = 0.3/12 × 100% = 2.5%

- Measured 98.2°C, true is 100.0°C
  Error = -1.8°C
  Percent error = 1.8/100 × 100% = 1.8%

- Measured 4.56 g, true is 4.50 g
  Error = 0.06 g
  Percent error = 0.06/4.5 × 100% = 1.33%

Steps:
1. Find absolute error: |measured - true|
2. Divide by true value
3. Multiply by 100%

Accepted percent error:
- In science, less than 5% is often acceptable
- Less than 1% indicates high accuracy
- More than 10% indicates problems"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[6]lvl3[12]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Error Propagation{note:"How errors combine in calculations.

Addition and subtraction:
- Add absolute uncertainties
- If A = B + C, δA = δB + δC
- Example: (12.3 ± 0.1) + (4.56 ± 0.05) = 16.86 ± 0.15

Multiplication and division:
- Add relative uncertainties
- If A = B × C, δA/A = δB/B + δC/C
- Example: (12.3 ± 0.1) × (4.56 ± 0.05)
  Relative: 0.1/12.3 = 0.0081, 0.05/4.56 = 0.0110
  Total relative = 0.0191 = 1.91%
  Absolute = 56.088 × 0.0191 = 1.07
  Result = 56.09 ± 1.07

Powers:
- If A = B^n, δA/A = n(δB/B)
- Example: (5.0 ± 0.1)²
  Relative: 2 × 0.1/5.0 = 0.04 = 4%
  Absolute: 25 × 0.04 = 1.0
  Result = 25.0 ± 1.0

Constants:
- Constants have no uncertainty
- Example: π = 3.14159... (exact)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]Pre-Algebra Concepts{note:"Pre-algebra introduces algebraic thinking: using variables to represent unknown quantities, forming and manipulating expressions, solving simple equations, and understanding inequalities. These concepts are the gateway to all higher mathematics."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[1]Variables{note:"A variable is a symbol (usually a letter) that represents an unknown quantity. It can hold any value. Examples: x, y, z, a, b, n. Variables allow us to generalize arithmetic and express relationships in a concise, powerful way. They are fundamental to algebra and all higher math."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing variables and their use in expressions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Variables as Unknowns{note:"Variables represent unknown values in equations.

Basic equations:
- 3x = 15 → x = 5
- 2y + 7 = 13 → 2y = 6 → y = 3
- 4z - 3 = 9 → 4z = 12 → z = 3
- 5a + 2 = 22 → 5a = 20 → a = 4

Variables can represent any number:
- x can be 2, 5, or 100
- 3x + 4 means 3 times the value of x plus 4

Example:
- If x = 2: 3x + 4 = 10
- If x = 5: 3x + 4 = 19
- If x = 10: 3x + 4 = 34

Using variables:
- Instead of saying "a number plus 5"
- We write x + 5
- The value changes based on what x is"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Variables in Formulas{note:"Variables are used in formulas to express relationships.

Area of rectangle:
- A = l × w
- A, l, w are variables
- If l=5 and w=3, then A=15

Speed:
- d = r × t
- d, r, t are variables
- If r=60 and t=2, then d=120

Temperature conversion:
- F = 1.8C + 32
- F and C are variables
- If C=20, then F=68

Perimeter:
- P = 2(l + w)
- P, l, w are variables
- If l=10 and w=5, then P=30

Volume:
- V = l × w × h
- V, l, w, h are variables
- If l=4, w=3, h=2, then V=24

Variables allow formulas to work for any values."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[1]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Variables in Patterns{note:"Variables represent terms in sequences.

Sequence: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, ...
- The nth term is 2n
- If n=5, term=10
- If n=10, term=20

Sequence: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, ...
- The nth term is n²
- If n=6, term=36
- If n=10, term=100

Sequence: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, ...
- The nth term is 3n
- If n=7, term=21
- If n=12, term=36

Variables in expressions:
- 2x + 5 means 2 times a number plus 5
- 3n - 2 means 3 times a number minus 2
- x² + 1 means a number squared plus 1

Examples:
- If x=3: 2x+5 = 11
- If n=4: 3n-2 = 10
- If x=5: x²+1 = 26"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[2]Expressions{note:"An algebraic expression combines variables, numbers, and operations (+, -, ×, ÷). It does not have an equals sign. Examples: 3x + 4, 2a - 7, 5y² + 2y - 1. Expressions are evaluated by substituting values for variables. They represent quantities that may vary."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing algebraic expressions and their evaluation."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Evaluating Expressions{note:"Substitute values for variables and simplify.

Examples:
- 3x + 4 when x=5: 3×5+4 = 19
- 2a - 7 when a=10: 20-7 = 13
- 5y² + 2y - 1 when y=3: 5×9+6-1 = 50
- 4x - 3 when x=2.5: 10-3 = 7
- 3n + 7 when n=4: 12+7 = 19
- 6x - 2 when x=3: 18-2 = 16
- 4a² when a=5: 4×25 = 100
- 2x + 3y when x=3, y=4: 6+12 = 18

Multiple variables:
- Evaluate 2a + 3b - c when a=2, b=4, c=5
  2×2 + 3×4 - 5 = 4+12-5 = 11

- Evaluate x² + y² when x=3, y=4
  9+16 = 25"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Terms and Coefficients{note:"Identify parts of an expression.

Expression: 3x + 4y - 7
- Terms: 3x, 4y, -7
- Coefficient of x: 3
- Coefficient of y: 4
- Constant term: -7

Expression: 2a² - 5a + 3
- Terms: 2a², -5a, +3
- Coefficient of a²: 2
- Coefficient of a: -5
- Constant: 3

Expression: 4x² + 3x - 2
- Coefficient of x²: 4
- Coefficient of x: 3
- Constant: -2

Expression: 7 - 3y + 2y²
- Terms: 7, -3y, 2y²
- Coefficient of y²: 2
- Coefficient of y: -3
- Constant: 7

Identifying parts:
- Terms are separated by + and - signs
- Coefficients are the numbers multiplying variables
- Constants are terms without variables"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[2]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Translating Words to Expressions{note:"Convert word phrases to algebraic expressions.

Addition:
- 'Sum of x and 5' = x + 5
- 'x plus 7' = x + 7
- '5 more than y' = y + 5

Subtraction:
- 'Difference of x and 3' = x - 3
- '5 less than y' = y - 5
- 'x minus 2' = x - 2

Multiplication:
- 'Product of 3 and y' = 3y
- 'Twice x' = 2x
- '3 times n' = 3n

Division:
- 'Quotient of x and 2' = x/2
- 'x divided by 3' = x/3

Combinations:
- '5 less than twice x' = 2x - 5
- 'Square of n plus 4' = n² + 4
- '3 times the sum of x and 2' = 3(x+2)
- 'Product of x and y' = xy
- 'Sum of a and b divided by 2' = (a+b)/2
- '5 more than 3 times x' = 3x + 5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]Equations{note:"An equation states that two expressions are equal, containing an equals sign. Example: 3x + 4 = 19. Solving an equation means finding the value(s) of the variable that make the equation true. Equations are used to model real-world problems and find unknown quantities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing equations and how to solve them."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Solving Basic Equations{note:"Isolate the variable using inverse operations.

Examples:
- 3x + 4 = 19 → 3x = 15 → x = 5
- 2a - 7 = 13 → 2a = 20 → a = 10
- 5y/2 = 10 → 5y = 20 → y = 4
- 4z + 3 = 19 → 4z = 16 → z = 4
- 3n - 5 = 16 → 3n = 21 → n = 7
- x/4 + 2 = 6 → x/4 = 4 → x = 16

Check by substitution:
- 3(5)+4 = 19 ✓
- 2(10)-7 = 13 ✓
- 5(4)/2 = 10 ✓
- 4(4)+3 = 19 ✓

Inverse operations:
- Addition ↔ Subtraction
- Multiplication ↔ Division
- Use inverse operations to isolate variable"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Multi-Step Equations{note:"Equations requiring multiple steps.

Examples:
- 4x + 7 = 3x + 12
  4x - 3x = 12 - 7
  x = 5

- 3(y-2) = 2y + 1
  3y - 6 = 2y + 1
  3y - 2y = 1 + 6
  y = 7

- 2(x+5) = 16
  2x + 10 = 16
  2x = 6
  x = 3

- 5(x-3) = 20
  5x - 15 = 20
  5x = 35
  x = 7

- 4x - 3 = 2x + 9
  4x - 2x = 9 + 3
  2x = 12
  x = 6

Strategy:
1. Simplify both sides (distribute, combine like terms)
2. Move variables to one side
3. Move constants to the other side
4. Divide by coefficient"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Equations with Fractions{note:"Solve equations containing fractions.

Examples:
- x/3 + 2 = 6 → x/3 = 4 → x = 12
- 2x/5 - 1 = 3 → 2x/5 = 4 → 2x = 20 → x = 10
- (x+2)/4 = 3 → x+2 = 12 → x = 10
- 3x/4 = 9 → 3x = 36 → x = 12
- (2x-1)/3 = 5 → 2x-1 = 15 → 2x = 16 → x = 8

Clear fractions by multiplying:
- x/2 + 1/3 = 1
  Multiply by 6 (LCD): 3x + 2 = 6
  3x = 4 → x = 4/3

- (2x)/3 + (x)/4 = 5
  Multiply by 12: 8x + 3x = 60
  11x = 60 → x = 60/11

- (x+1)/2 = (x-1)/3
  Cross multiply: 3(x+1) = 2(x-1)
  3x+3 = 2x-2
  x = -5"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[3]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Real-World Equations{note:"Equations model real-world situations.

Cost:
- 2x + 5 = 15 (2 items plus $5 shipping = $15)
  2x = 10 → x = 5
  Each item costs $5

Age:
- x + (x+4) = 28 (two people, one 4 years older)
  2x + 4 = 28 → 2x = 24 → x = 12
  Ages: 12 and 16

Perimeter:
- 2(l + 5) = 24 (rectangle with width 5, perimeter 24)
  2l + 10 = 24 → 2l = 14 → l = 7
  Length is 7

Distance:
- 60t = 240 (traveling at 60 mph for t hours)
  t = 240/60 = 4 hours

Money:
- 0.05x + 0.10(10) = 1.00 (nickels and dimes)
  0.05x + 1.00 = 1.00 → 0.05x = 0 → x = 0
  No nickels"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[4]Inequalities{note:"An inequality compares two expressions using <, >, ≤, ≥, or ≠. Example: 2x + 3 < 13. Solving inequalities is similar to equations, but multiplying/dividing by a negative number reverses the inequality sign. Inequalities represent ranges of values and are used in optimization, constraints, and modeling real-world conditions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing inequalities and how to solve them."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Solving Basic Inequalities{note:"Solve inequalities by isolating the variable.

Examples:
- 2x + 3 < 13 → 2x < 10 → x < 5
- 3y - 7 ≥ 8 → 3y ≥ 15 → y ≥ 5
- 5 - 2z > 11 → -2z > 6 → z < -3 (flip sign)
- 4x + 1 ≤ 9 → 4x ≤ 8 → x ≤ 2
- 3x + 2 > 11 → 3x > 9 → x > 3
- 5x - 3 ≤ 12 → 5x ≤ 15 → x ≤ 3

Important rule:
- When multiplying or dividing by a negative number
- Flip the inequality sign
- Example: -2z > 6 → z < -3

Check solutions:
- x < 5: any x less than 5 works
- y ≥ 5: 5, 6, 7, ... work

Graphing:
- < or >: open circle
- ≤ or ≥: closed circle
- x < 5: open circle at 5, line left
- y ≥ 5: closed circle at 5, line right"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Compound Inequalities{note:"Inequalities with two conditions.

Examples:
- -3 < 2x + 1 < 7
  -3-1 < 2x < 7-1
  -4 < 2x < 6
  -2 < x < 3

- x ≤ 4 and x > -1 → -1 < x ≤ 4
  Open at -1, closed at 4

- 3x - 2 > 7 or 2x + 1 < 9
  3x > 9 or 2x < 8
  x > 3 or x < 4
  Solution: all real numbers (since 3 < 4)

- -2 ≤ x ≤ 4
  Closed at -2 and 4

- 0 < x < 5
  Open at 0 and 5

Graphing compound:
- -2 < x ≤ 3: open at -2, closed at 3
- x < -1 or x > 2: open at -1 line left, open at 2 line right

Interval notation:
- -2 < x < 3 = (-2, 3)
- -2 ≤ x ≤ 3 = [-2, 3]
- x > 3 = (3, ∞)
- x ≤ 4 = (-∞, 4]
- x < -1 or x > 2 = (-∞, -1) ∪ (2, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[4]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Real-World Inequalities{note:"Inequalities in everyday situations.

Budget:
- You have $50 to spend on items costing $x each
- 3x ≤ 50 → x ≤ 16.67
- You can buy items costing up to $16.67

Temperature:
- Water freezes below 0°C
- T < 0°C for ice
- T > 100°C for boiling

Age:
- To vote, age ≥ 18
- a ≥ 18

Speed limit:
- Speed ≤ 65 mph
- s ≤ 65

Minimum requirements:
- Must have at least 5 hours of sleep
- h ≥ 5

Maximum capacity:
- A room can hold at most 50 people
- p ≤ 50

Height requirements:
- Must be at least 48 inches to ride
- h ≥ 48

Grade requirements:
- Need at least 70% to pass
- g ≥ 70"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]Simple Linear Equations{note:"A linear equation is an equation of the form ax + b = c, where a, b, c are constants and a≠0. They have one solution. Linear equations can be solved by isolating the variable using inverse operations. They model many real-world situations like cost, distance, and uniform motion."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing simple linear equations and solving methods."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Linear Equations{note:"Solve one-step and two-step linear equations.

One-step:
- x + 7 = 12 → x = 5
- y - 4 = 9 → y = 13
- 3x = 21 → x = 7
- x/4 = 6 → x = 24

Two-step:
- 2x + 5 = 13 → 2x = 8 → x = 4
- 3y - 7 = 20 → 3y = 27 → y = 9
- 5z/2 = 15 → 5z = 30 → z = 6
- 4a - 3 = 17 → 4a = 20 → a = 5

Check:
- x=5: 5+7=12 ✓
- y=13: 13-4=9 ✓
- x=7: 3×7=21 ✓
- x=24: 24/4=6 ✓

Inverse operations:
- Addition ↔ Subtraction
- Multiplication ↔ Division
- Use opposite operations to isolate variable"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Variables on Both Sides{note:"Equations with variables on both sides.

Examples:
- 3x + 4 = 2x + 10
  3x - 2x = 10 - 4
  x = 6

- 5y - 3 = 2y + 9
  5y - 2y = 9 + 3
  3y = 12
  y = 4

- 7x + 2 = 3x + 18
  7x - 3x = 18 - 2
  4x = 16
  x = 4

- 8x - 3 = 5x + 12
  8x - 5x = 12 + 3
  3x = 15
  x = 5

- 6x - 7 = 2x + 5
  6x - 2x = 5 + 7
  4x = 12
  x = 3

Strategy:
1. Move variables to one side
2. Move constants to the other side
3. Divide by coefficient

Check:
- 3(6)+4 = 2(6)+10 → 22 = 22 ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Real-World Linear Equations{note:"Applications of linear equations.

Cost:
- 3x + 5 = 20 (3 items plus $5 = $20)
  x = 5 (each item costs $5)

Age:
- x + (x+5) = 35 (two people, one 5 years older)
  2x + 5 = 35 → 2x = 30 → x = 15
  Ages: 15 and 20

Perimeter:
- 2(l + 5) = 24 (rectangle with width 5, perimeter 24)
  2l + 10 = 24 → 2l = 14 → l = 7
  Length is 7

Distance:
- 60t = 180 (traveling at 60 mph for t hours)
  t = 3 hours

Money:
- 0.25x + 0.10(15) = 5.00 (quarters and dimes)
  0.25x + 1.50 = 5.00 → 0.25x = 3.50 → x = 14 quarters

Work:
- 2x + 3x = 40 (two workers with different rates)
  5x = 40 → x = 8 hours

Temperature:
- F = 1.8C + 32 (convert 68°F to C)
  68 = 1.8C + 32 → 36 = 1.8C → C = 20°C"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[5]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Graphing Linear Equations{note:"Linear equations can be graphed as straight lines.

Slope-intercept form: y = mx + b
- m = slope (rate of change)
- b = y-intercept (where x=0)

Examples:
- y = 2x + 3
  Slope = 2, y-intercept = 3
  Points: (0,3), (1,5), (2,7)

- y = -x + 4
  Slope = -1, y-intercept = 4
  Points: (0,4), (1,3), (2,2)

- y = 0.5x - 2
  Slope = 0.5, y-intercept = -2
  Points: (0,-2), (2,-1), (4,0)

Graphing steps:
1. Plot y-intercept (0,b)
2. Use slope to find another point
3. Draw line through points

Finding slope:
- m = (y₂-y₁)/(x₂-x₁)
- Between (1,3) and (2,5): m = (5-3)/(2-1) = 2

Special cases:
- y = 3 (horizontal line, slope=0)
- x = 2 (vertical line, undefined slope)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]Graphing on Number Lines{note:"Number line graphing represents solutions to equations and inequalities visually. A closed circle (●) shows inclusion (≤, ≥), an open circle (○) shows exclusion (<, >). Number lines are essential for understanding intervals, solving compound inequalities, and visualizing solution sets in pre-algebra and algebra."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing number line graphing for equations and inequalities."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Number Line Graphs{note:"Graphing equations and inequalities on a number line.

Equation:
- x = 3: closed circle at 3 only

Inequalities:
- x > 3: open circle at 3, line to right
- x < 3: open circle at 3, line to left
- x ≥ 3: closed circle at 3, line to right
- x ≤ 3: closed circle at 3, line to left

Examples:
- x > -2: open at -2, line right
- x ≤ 1: closed at 1, line left
- x < 0: open at 0, line left
- x ≥ -1: closed at -1, line right

Symbols:
- > or < : open circle (not included)
- ≥ or ≤ : closed circle (included)
- = : closed circle at point

Always use open/closed circles correctly."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Compound Inequalities on Number Lines{note:"Graphing compound inequalities.

"and" (intersection):
- -2 < x < 4: open at -2 and 4, line between
- -2 ≤ x ≤ 4: closed at -2 and 4, line between
- -2 < x ≤ 4: open at -2, closed at 4, line between
- -2 ≤ x < 4: closed at -2, open at 4, line between

"or" (union):
- x ≤ -1 or x > 3: closed at -1 line left, open at 3 line right
- x < -2 or x ≥ 1: open at -2 line left, closed at 1 line right
- x < 0 or x > 5: open at 0 line left, open at 5 line right

Examples:
- 0 < x ≤ 5: open at 0, closed at 5
- -3 ≤ x < 2: closed at -3, open at 2
- x < -1 or x ≥ 4: open at -1 left, closed at 4 right
- -5 < x < -1: open at -5 and -1

Visualizing:
- "and": overlapping region (between)
- "or": union of regions (outside and between)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Interval Notation{note:"Interval notation is a concise way to describe sets on the number line.

Types:
- (a, b): open interval (a < x < b)
- [a, b]: closed interval (a ≤ x ≤ b)
- (a, b]: half-open (a < x ≤ b)
- [a, b): half-open (a ≤ x < b)
- (a, ∞): x > a
- [a, ∞): x ≥ a
- (-∞, b): x < b
- (-∞, b]: x ≤ b
- (-∞, ∞): all real numbers

Examples:
- x > 3 = (3, ∞)
- x ≤ 4 = (-∞, 4]
- -2 < x < 4 = (-2, 4)
- -2 ≤ x ≤ 4 = [-2, 4]
- x ≤ -1 or x > 3 = (-∞, -1] ∪ (3, ∞)
- 0 < x ≤ 5 = (0, 5]

Special cases:
- All real numbers: (-∞, ∞)
- Empty set: ∅ or {}

Using ∪ for union:
- x < -1 or x > 2 = (-∞, -1) ∪ (2, ∞)
- x ≤ 0 or x ≥ 5 = (-∞, 0] ∪ [5, ∞)"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[6]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Absolute Value on Number Lines{note:"Graphing absolute value equations and inequalities.

|x| = a:
- x = a or x = -a
- Points at -a and a

|x| < a:
- -a < x < a
- Open at -a and a, line between

|x| ≤ a:
- -a ≤ x ≤ a
- Closed at -a and a, line between

|x| > a:
- x < -a or x > a
- Open at -a line left, open at a line right

|x| ≥ a:
- x ≤ -a or x ≥ a
- Closed at -a line left, closed at a line right

Examples:
- |x| = 3: points at -3 and 3
- |x| < 3: (-3, 3)
- |x| ≥ 2: (-∞, -2] ∪ [2, ∞)
- |x - 1| < 2: -2 < x-1 < 2 → -1 < x < 3
- |x + 2| ≤ 3: -3 ≤ x+2 ≤ 3 → -5 ≤ x ≤ 1

Visual:
- |x| < 3: between -3 and 3
- |x| > 3: outside -3 and 3"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]Combining Like Terms{note:"Combining like terms simplifies expressions by adding or subtracting terms with the same variable and exponent. Example: 3x + 2x = 5x, 4y² - 2y² = 2y². Like terms have identical variable parts. This is fundamental for simplifying expressions and solving equations efficiently."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing combining like terms in expressions."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Basic Like Terms{note:"Combine terms with the same variable.

Examples:
- 3x + 2x = 5x
- 7y - 3y = 4y
- 4a + 5a - 2a = 7a
- 8b² - 3b² = 5b²
- 9x + 4x = 13x
- 6y - 2y = 4y
- 3a + 7a - 5a = 5a
- 11z - 4z = 7z

Multiple variables:
- 5x + 2y - 3x + 4y = 2x + 6y
- 7a + 3b - 2a - 4b = 5a - b
- 6x + 4y + 2x - 3y = 8x + y
- 9m - 5n + 3m + 2n = 12m - 3n

Rule: Only combine terms with identical variable parts.
- 3x and 5x are like terms
- 3x and 5y are not like terms
- 3x² and 5x² are like terms
- 3x² and 5x are not like terms"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]With Constants{note:"Combine terms with constants.

Examples:
- 3x + 4 + 2x - 1 = 5x + 3
- 7y - 2 + 3y + 5 = 10y + 3
- 4a² - 3a + 2a² + 5a - 1 = 6a² + 2a - 1
- 5x - 3 + 2x + 7 - 4x = 3x + 4
- 2x² + 3x - x² + 5x - 2 = x² + 8x - 2
- 8y + 5 - 2y - 3 = 6y + 2
- 6a - 4 + 3a + 7 = 9a + 3

Strategy:
1. Group like terms together
2. Combine variable terms
3. Combine constant terms

Example:
5x - 3 + 2x + 7 - 4x
= (5x + 2x - 4x) + (-3 + 7)
= 3x + 4"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]With Parentheses{note:"Simplify expressions with parentheses.

Examples:
- 2(x+3) + 3(x-1) = 2x+6+3x-3 = 5x+3
- 4(2y-1) - 3(y+2) = 8y-4-3y-6 = 5y-10
- 3(2a+5) + 2(4a-3) = 6a+15+8a-6 = 14a+9
- 5(x+2) - 3(x-4) = 5x+10-3x+12 = 2x+22
- 2(3x-1) + 4(x+2) = 6x-2+4x+8 = 10x+6
- 3(2y+5) - 2(4y-3) = 6y+15-8y+6 = -2y+21

Steps:
1. Distribute (multiply through parentheses)
2. Combine like terms
3. Simplify

Example:
5(x+2) - 3(x-4)
= 5x+10 - 3x+12
= (5x-3x) + (10+12)
= 2x+22

Checking:
- Distributing carefully
- Watch signs with negatives"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[7]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Like Terms Identification{note:"Identifying like terms correctly.

Like terms must have:
1. Same variable (letter)
2. Same exponent (power)

Examples of like terms:
- 3x and 5x (same variable x, same exponent 1)
- 2x² and 7x² (same variable x, same exponent 2)
- 4xy and 6xy (same variables x and y, same exponents)
- 5a²b and 3a²b (same variables a²b)

Not like terms:
- 3x and 4x² (different exponents)
- 5x and 3y (different variables)
- 2xy and 3x (different variable combinations)
- 4x² and 5x (different exponents)

Combining like terms:
- 3x² + 5x² = 8x²
- 4xy + 6xy = 10xy
- 3x + 4x² (cannot combine)
- 5a²b + 3a²b = 8a²b

Constants:
- All constants are like terms
- 3 + 5 - 2 = 6
- 4x + 3 - 2x + 5 = 2x + 8"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]Distributive Property{note:"The distributive property: a(b+c) = ab + ac. It allows multiplication to be distributed over addition or subtraction. This property is fundamental in algebra for expanding expressions, factoring, and solving equations. Example: 3(2x + 4) = 6x + 12."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing the distributive property in action."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Expanding Expressions{note:"Use distribution to expand expressions.

Basic:
- 3(x+4) = 3x + 12
- 5(2y-3) = 10y - 15
- -2(3a+4) = -6a - 8
- 4(2x-5) = 8x - 20
- 7(3x+2) = 21x + 14
- 6(4y-2) = 24y - 12

With variables:
- x(x+3) = x² + 3x
- 2x(3x-4) = 6x² - 8x
- 3a(2a+5) = 6a² + 15a
- -y(y-4) = -y² + 4y
- 4x(2x+3) = 8x² + 12x

With negatives:
- -3(x-2) = -3x + 6
- -2(4y-5) = -8y + 10
- -(3x-7) = -3x + 7
- -4(2a-3b) = -8a + 12b

Rule: a(b+c) = ab + ac
Watch signs carefully!"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Factoring with Distributive Property{note:"Use the distributive property in reverse to factor.

GCF factoring:
- 6x + 9 = 3(2x+3)
- 10y - 25 = 5(2y-5)
- 14a + 21 = 7(2a+3)
- 8x - 12 = 4(2x-3)
- 15y + 20 = 5(3y+4)
- 18x + 27 = 9(2x+3)

With variables:
- 4x² + 6x = 2x(2x+3)
- 9a² - 12a = 3a(3a-4)
- 12x³ + 18x² = 6x²(2x+3)
- 15y² - 20y = 5y(3y-4)
- 24x² + 16x = 8x(3x+2)

Steps:
1. Find the GCF of all terms
2. Write GCF outside parentheses
3. Divide each term by GCF
4. Write result inside parentheses

Check: Multiply back to verify."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Solving Equations with Distribution{note:"Use the distributive property to solve equations.

Examples:
- 3(2x+4) = 18
  6x + 12 = 18
  6x = 6
  x = 1

- 2(3x-1) = 10
  6x - 2 = 10
  6x = 12
  x = 2

- 4(x+2) = 20
  4x + 8 = 20
  4x = 12
  x = 3

- 5(2x-3) = 25
  10x - 15 = 25
  10x = 40
  x = 4

- 3(x+4) - 2x = 14
  3x + 12 - 2x = 14
  x + 12 = 14
  x = 2

- 2(3x-1) + x = 12
  6x - 2 + x = 12
  7x - 2 = 12
  7x = 14
  x = 2

Strategy:
1. Distribute first
2. Combine like terms
3. Solve the resulting equation"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[8]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Special Distributive Cases{note:"Special cases of the distributive property.

Negative distribution:
- -(2x + 3) = -2x - 3
- -(4y - 5) = -4y + 5
- -(3a + 2b) = -3a - 2b

Multiple terms:
- 3(2x - 4y + 5) = 6x - 12y + 15
- -2(3x + 2y - 4) = -6x - 4y + 8
- 4(2a - 3b + c) = 8a - 12b + 4c

With exponents:
- 2x(3x² + 4x - 5) = 6x³ + 8x² - 10x
- 3y²(2y - 4) = 6y³ - 12y²
- 4a²(3a - 2a²) = 12a³ - 8a⁴

Fraction distribution:
- (1/2)(4x + 6) = 2x + 3
- (2/3)(9y - 6) = 6y - 4
- (3/4)(8x + 4) = 6x + 3

Always multiply every term inside parentheses.
Be careful with signs."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]Factoring Basics{note:"Factoring is the reverse of expanding. It rewrites an expression as a product of simpler expressions. Example: 6x + 15 = 3(2x + 5). Factoring is essential for simplifying expressions, solving equations, and working with fractions. It is the foundation of many algebraic techniques."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]Examples{note:"Examples showing basic factoring techniques."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[1]Factoring Out GCF{note:"Find and factor out the greatest common factor.

Examples:
- 12x + 18 = 6(2x+3)
  GCF = 6
- 15y - 25 = 5(3y-5)
  GCF = 5
- 14a + 21 = 7(2a+3)
  GCF = 7
- 8x - 12 = 4(2x-3)
  GCF = 4

With variables:
- 8a² + 12a = 4a(2a+3)
  GCF = 4a
- 18x³ - 27x² = 9x²(2x-3)
  GCF = 9x²
- 24x² + 36x = 12x(2x+3)
  GCF = 12x

Steps:
1. Find the GCF of all terms
2. Write GCF outside parentheses
3. Divide each term by GCF
4. Write result inside parentheses

Check by multiplying back:
- 6(2x+3) = 12x+18 ✓
- 4a(2a+3) = 8a²+12a ✓"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[2]Factoring by Grouping{note:"Group terms to find common factors.

Examples:
- 2x² + 4x + 3x + 6
  Group: (2x²+4x) + (3x+6)
  Factor: 2x(x+2) + 3(x+2)
  = (2x+3)(x+2)

- 3y² + 6y + 4y + 8
  Group: (3y²+6y) + (4y+8)
  Factor: 3y(y+2) + 4(y+2)
  = (3y+4)(y+2)

- 6x² + 9x + 4x + 6
  Group: (6x²+9x) + (4x+6)
  Factor: 3x(2x+3) + 2(2x+3)
  = (3x+2)(2x+3)

- 4a² + 6a + 2a + 3
  Group: (4a²+6a) + (2a+3)
  Factor: 2a(2a+3) + 1(2a+3)
  = (2a+1)(2a+3)

Steps:
1. Group terms in pairs
2. Factor out GCF from each pair
3. Factor out common binomial
4. Verify by multiplying"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[3]Factoring Quadratics (x² + bx + c){note:"Factor trinomials of the form x² + bx + c.

Examples:
- x² + 5x + 6 = (x+2)(x+3)
  Factors of 6 that sum to 5: 2+3=5

- x² - 5x + 6 = (x-2)(x-3)
  Factors of 6 that sum to -5: -2-3=-5

- x² + x - 6 = (x+3)(x-2)
  Factors of -6 that sum to 1: 3-2=1

- x² - x - 6 = (x-3)(x+2)
  Factors of -6 that sum to -1: -3+2=-1

- x² + 7x + 12 = (x+3)(x+4)
  Factors of 12: 3+4=7

- x² - 7x + 12 = (x-3)(x-4)
  Factors of 12: -3-4=-7

Steps:
1. Find two numbers that multiply to c
2. The two numbers must add to b
3. Write as (x + first)(x + second)
4. Check signs carefully"}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[4]Factoring Quadratics (ax² + bx + c){note:"Factor trinomials of the form ax² + bx + c.

Examples:
- 2x² + 7x + 3 = (2x+1)(x+3)
  Check: 2x×3 + 1×x = 6x+x = 7x ✓

- 3x² - 10x + 3 = (3x-1)(x-3)
  Check: 3x×(-3) + (-1)×x = -9x-x = -10x ✓

- 6x² + 5x - 4 = (3x+4)(2x-1)
  Check: 3x×(-1) + 4×2x = -3x+8x = 5x ✓

- 4x² - 4x - 15 = (2x-5)(2x+3)
  Check: 2x×3 + (-5)×2x = 6x-10x = -4x ✓

- 6x² - 7x - 3 = (3x+1)(2x-3)
  Check: 3x×(-3) + 1×2x = -9x+2x = -7x ✓

Methods:
- Trial and error
- AC method
- Grouping method

Always verify by multiplying back."}

lvl1[1]lvl2[7]lvl3[9]lvl4[1]lvl5[5]Special Factoring Patterns{note:"Recognize and factor special patterns.

Difference of squares:
- a² - b² = (a+b)(a-b)
- x² - 9 = (x+3)(x-3)
- 4x² - 25 = (2x+5)(2x-5)
- 9y² - 16 = (3y+4)(3y-4)
- 25a² - 4 = (5a+2)(5a-2)

Perfect square trinomials:
- a² + 2ab + b² = (a+b)²
- x² + 6x + 9 = (x+3)²
- 4x² + 12x + 9 = (2x+3)²

- a² - 2ab + b² = (a-b)²
- x² - 10x + 25 = (x-5)²
- 9x² - 12x + 4 = (3x-2)²

Sum/difference of cubes:
- a³ + b³ = (a+b)(a²-ab+b²)
  x³ + 8 = (x+2)(x²-2x+4)

- a³ - b³ = (a-b)(a²+ab+b²)
  x³ - 27 = (x-3)(x²+3x+9)

Recognizing these patterns saves time."}
